Writing Code vs. Shipping Code: Productivity Effects Across Generations of AI Coding Tools
Mert Demirer, Leon Musolff, and Liyuan Yang
NBER Working Paper No. 35275
May 2026, Revised September 2026
JEL No. D24, L86, O33

                                          ABSTRACT

How do the productivity effects of AI evolve across successive generations of tools, and to what
extent do task-level gains ultimately translate into final output? We study these questions in the
context of software development, using data on more than 500,000 GitHub developers combined
with their AI usage telemetry. In a matched event study design, we find that autocomplete,
interactive coding agents, and autonomous coding agents each significantly increase coding activity
(“commits”), with respective cumulative effects of 30%, 180%, and 240%. These gains, however,
attenuate sharply across the production hierarchy: the 240% cumulative effect falls to 80% for
the number of projects, and to 30% for actual releases. This pattern is consistent with the weak-
link hypothesis: the strong productivity gains from AI are attenuated by human bottlenecks
in the production chain, with an estimated elasticity of substitution of 0.23 between AI and human
effort, which indicates strong complementarities. We further confirm these results across four
major software marketplaces, finding a sharp increase in the number of new apps but no increase
in total usage. Large task-level AI productivity gains have therefore translated only partially into
shipped and used software thus far.

Mert Demirer                                     Liyuan Yang
Massachusetts Institute of Technology            Massachusetts Institute of Technology
Department of Economics                          lyyang@mit.edu

1   Introduction
Generative AI differs from earlier technologies in how fast it has improved and diffused:
model capabilities have advanced rapidly, and firms deploy AI across a growing range of
applications (Bick et al., 2025; Kalyani et al., 2025; Humlum and Vestergaard, 2025; Yotzov
et al., 2026). While much of the existing literature emphasizes the productivity effects of
individual AI tools on specific tasks, far less is known about two questions that are central
to understanding the economic impact of AI. How do productivity effects evolve across
generations of tools? And how much of the task-level gains reaches final output?
   We study these questions in software development, one of the earliest and most promi-
nent domains of AI adoption (Handa et al., 2025) and among the occupations most exposed
to AI (Felten et al., 2023; Eloundou et al., 2024; Tomlinson et al., 2025). Software devel-
opment provides an ideal setting for two reasons. First, it has seen three generations
of widely adopted tools: autocomplete, which suggests code as the developer types; sync
agents, which write and edit code alongside the developer in real time; and async agents,
which can be assigned a task and work autonomously until it is complete. Second, its pro-
duction process is a well-defined sequence of stages—writing code, integrating changes
across files, reviewing and merging pull requests, managing releases—so we can measure
productivity at each point along the chain.
   Our first contribution is to the literature on how AI affects productivity in specific
tasks (Noy and Zhang, 2023; Brynjolfsson et al., 2025; Cui et al., 2026; Dell’Acqua et al.,
2026). We provide evidence from more than 500,000 developers on GitHub across multiple
tools and four years of model improvement (2022–2026), so we track how effects evolve as
capabilities advance rather than at a single point in time.
     Our second contribution is to measure how much of these gains reaches final output.
Task-level estimates of AI’s productivity effect are large (15–50%, see the review by Fruits
and Stout 2026), but several papers argue they need not translate into proportional gains in
aggregate output (Acemoglu, 2025; Jones, 2026; Karger et al., 2026). The leading argument
is the bottleneck or weak-links hypothesis: AI raises productivity in some tasks, but output
is limited by complementary tasks that humans still perform at unchanged productivity
(Kremer, 1993; Jones, 2011, 2026). We extend the weak-links framework to a hierarchical
model in which software output is produced by sequentially aggregating tasks within a
single production process, and provide one of the first empirical tests of the hypothesis.
    In the model, output at each layer combines the previous layer’s output with human
input at that layer through a CES function. AI can affect a layer in two ways: by augmenting
human input, so each unit of human effort produces more, or by partially automating
it, so direct human effort is no longer needed but human review still is. For instance,

autocomplete augments the production of code but does not automate it; by contrast,
async agents automate not only the production of code but the production of entire pull
requests—which still require human review before they can be merged. The key parameter
in the model is the elasticity of substitution. The lower it is, the more AI’s gains are
bottlenecked by the human stages downstream and the more they attenuate along the
production chain. In the case of complementarities, even unbounded automation yields
only finite gains, as in the weak-links literature (Kremer, 1993; Jones, 2011).
    To take the model to the data, we combine public GitHub data with internal Microsoft
telemetry to measure activity at each layer of the hierarchy and to date each developer’s
adoption of AI tools. Combining publicly observable AI usage on GitHub with internal
Microsoft data lets us track multiple tools across vendors: autocomplete from GitHub
Copilot; sync agents from GitHub Copilot, Claude, and Codex; and async agents from
GitHub Copilot and Codex. To measure the productivity effects of each tool, we implement
an event-study design in which each treated user is matched with a similarly active control
user in the same calendar week one year earlier. Drawing controls from the prior year
avoids contamination from private AI use among contemporaneous non-adopters.
    Figure 1 summarizes our results, plotting the cumulative effect of each generation of
tools on six outcomes spanning the production hierarchy, from lines of code through
releases. Focusing on commits, our measure of task-level output, AI coding tools raise


                                                                                2
productivity substantially, and the effect grows with each generation. Autocomplete raises
weekly commits by about 30%; adding sync agents brings the cumulative effect to about
180%; adding async agents brings it to about 240%. These effects are larger for less active
developers, though they remain substantial throughout the activity distribution.
    How do these task-level gains translate into final output? Effects attenuate sharply up
the production hierarchy. Developers write much more code and work on more features
after adoption, but ship only modestly more software. Sync agents raise lines of code by
958% and pull requests by 86%, but releases by only 20%. Autocomplete shows the same
pattern: 234% on lines of code, 30% on commits, 9% on releases. Aggregate GitHub activity
corroborates the developer-level estimates: new repositories and pull requests have risen
sharply since early 2025, in a pattern consistent with the attenuation we estimate.
    We next examine two mechanisms that could generate this attenuation: an increase
in scope and experimentation. Under increased scope, the productivity gains do reach the
higher layers, but they accrue through an increase in the size of each unit of output rather
than in the number of units produced. Under experimentation, developers generate more
coding activity at the earlier stages, where AI is most effective, but most of it does not reach
final output. This is the natural implication of the complementarities in our model: code
review, quality control, and release capacity limit how much of the additional early-stage
output advances to the later stages, where human input remains essential.
    We find that increased scope accounts for only a small fraction of the attenuation and
that most of it reflects experimentation. The evidence is visible at every layer of the
hierarchy. After adopting sync agents, developers add 7.0 times as many lines of code
but delete 12.2 times as many, so more of what they write is rewritten or discarded. They
create 79% more branches, exploring features that are often later abandoned. And they
try more project ideas that go nowhere: the share of new repositories receiving no further
activity after their first month has risen 9 percentage points since 2021, from 60% to 69%.
    Having shown evidence consistent with complementarities, we next quantify them
directly by mapping our estimates to the model’s parameters. The estimated attenuation
pattern is consistent with an elasticity of substitution of 0.23 between upstream output
and human effort, indicating strong complementarity between AI and human inputs. The
estimates are also consistent with autocomplete entering the production hierarchy only at
the code-writing layer, while sync and async agents intervene at later stages as well. We also
investigate whether the empirical results are consistent with developers reallocating their
inputs across layers in response to AI, and find no evidence of full reoptimization. This
is consistent with the slow adjustment of production processes to new general-purpose
technologies (Bresnahan and Trajtenberg, 1995; Brynjolfsson et al., 2021).


                                               3
    Our evidence so far shows an increase in developer-side output, which does not tell
us whether new apps reach end users or how widely they are used. To address these
questions, we assemble monthly panels for four of the largest software marketplaces: the
Apple App Store, the Google Play Store, the Chrome Web Store, and SourceForge. For
each marketplace, we observe the first date that each app appeared and a measure of
cumulative end-user engagement (the number of ratings or downloads). With these data,
we quantify whether the number of new apps has increased over time and, if so, whether
this translates into greater usage.
    New apps have risen across all marketplaces since early 2025, with monthly entry
roughly doubling on average. Usage, however, has not followed: total engagement with
new apps after launch has not increased in any marketplace. While this rules out market
expansion effects of AI, one possibility is that these apps still raise consumer surplus
through better matching—consumers use or switch to niche apps that are better matched
to their tastes—even though total usage remains the same. Our evidence does not support
this hypothesis either: the share of new apps that fail to reach even a modest audience
has risen everywhere, so the expansion is concentrated in apps with little or no user base.
This pattern admits two interpretations: either the marginal apps are of low quality, or
there is an additional bottleneck on the consumer side—discovery and adoption of new
apps take time—that prevents supply-side gains from translating into usage. Our data
currently cannot distinguish between the two.
    We use several strategies to address the endogeneity concerns inherent in observational
data and to rule out alternative explanations for the attenuation pattern we document.
First, to mitigate activity bias, we require control users to be similarly active on the treated
user’s adoption date and match on pre-treatment activity over the prior eleven weeks.
Second, we validate our results with placebo tests that apply the same event-study design
to non-AI coding tools and find no effects. Third, we document the absence of differential
pre-trends between treated and control users across all outcomes, supporting the parallel
trends assumption. Fourth, we show that our results are robust to several choices in
matching and empirical specification. Fifth, we compare our autocomplete estimates with
the prior field-experimental evidence of Cui et al. (2026), who estimate the effect of the
same tool in the same time period. Sixth, we show that the productivity effects and the
attenuation are not driven by changes in what we measure: neither a decline in the quality
of commits nor an increase in the verbosity of AI-generated code accounts for them.
   Three caveats are worth noting about the external validity of our findings. First, our data
cover only open-source software and four major software marketplaces. While open-source
software is a large segment of the market and a key input into proprietary software (Nagle,


                                               4
2019; Gortmaker, 2026), our data have little coverage of enterprise software. However,
other research shows that the same attenuation pattern holds in enterprise software (Chen
and Stratton, 2026), with effects considerably smaller than ours. This is expected, as open-
source developers work in small teams and face less organizational friction, so realizing
productivity gains from AI requires less complementary investment (Brynjolfsson et al.,
2021). In this sense, we think our setting is a leading indicator of how AI will affect the
broader industry. Second, generative AI has advanced furthest in software engineering
relative to other domains. While this lets us test general principles about the impact of
AI, our quantitative estimates may not extrapolate to other domains. Third, although our
data are nearly contemporaneous, extending through mid-2026, our estimates necessarily
reflect the capabilities of the tools available to date. How much these bottlenecks loosen
going forward will depend on whether future tools need less human review or automate
higher levels of the hierarchy, and on how quickly they spread across stages of production.

Relation to Literature Our work relates to four strands of literature. First, a growing
literature studies the effects of generative AI tools on productivity across settings, including
coding (Peng et al., 2023; Kreitmeir and Raschky, 2024; Chen et al., 2025; Becker et al., 2025;
Song et al., 2025; Cui et al., 2026; Sarkar, 2026; He et al., 2026; Chen and Stratton, 2026),
customer support (Brynjolfsson et al., 2025), law (Choi and Schwarcz, 2025), consulting
(Dell’Acqua et al., 2026), writing (Noy and Zhang, 2023), entrepreneurship (Otis et al.,
2026), hiring (Jabarian and Henkel, 2026), and AI use in startups (Kim et al., 2026). With
some exceptions, these papers find productivity effects on the order of 15–50% or more
(Fruits and Stout, 2026). Other papers show that AI changes not only productivity but also
how workers allocate time across tasks: away from coordination and communication, and
toward individual production work (Hoffmann et al., 2024; Dillon et al., 2025). A related
literature studies aggregate labor-market outcomes and finds modest employment effects
to date (Hampole et al., 2025; Humlum and Vestergaard, 2025), though Brynjolfsson et al.
(2026) document declines for early-career workers in the most AI-exposed occupations.1
   Most of these estimates are for early-generation tools—autocomplete and chatbots—
while developers are shifting to agentic tools (Johnston et al., 2026). Systematic evidence
on agentic tools remains limited and mixed: Becker et al. (2025) find that AI coding tools
slow experienced developers down, while other studies report substantial productivity
gains (Chen et al., 2025; Sarkar, 2026). Closest to our paper, Chen and Stratton (2026) find
that AI tools raise coding activity in enterprises, but that these gains only partially pass
1Relatedly, Autor and Thompson (2025) and Freund and Mann (2026) study how automation reshapes
 occupations by changing the tasks that remain—through the expertise required by remaining tasks in
 the former, and through worker sorting across occupations by comparative advantage in the latter.



                                                 5
through to output. The papers differ in four ways. They study firms; we study open-source
development. Their evidence on the bottleneck is direct, coming from longer review times,
whereas we quantify the strength of complementarities by developing and calibrating a
model of software production. They examine labor market effects, whereas we examine the
demand side, asking whether the additional software reaches users. Finally, we emphasize
how effects change across three generations of tools as the technology improves.
    Second, our conceptual framework builds on the task-based approach to automation
(Autor et al., 2003; Acemoglu and Autor, 2011; Acemoglu and Restrepo, 2018, 2019, 2022), in
which AI displaces human labor in some tasks but not others. In this literature, Acemoglu
(2025) considers how task-level productivity gains aggregate to economy-wide output, and
Demirer et al. (2026), Gans and Goldfarb (2026), Garicano et al. (2026), and Agrawal et al.
(2026) consider how automation—or augmentation—propagates through interdependen-
cies and chains of tasks.2 When tasks are complementary, automating a subset of them
has bounded effects on aggregate output—the “weak links” or “O-ring” logic of Kremer
(1993) and Jones (2011), which Aghion et al. (2019) and Jones (2026) extend to AI. We apply
this logic vertically within a single production process: the “tasks” are the layers of the
software production hierarchy, and the weak link is the human-bottlenecked layer with
the smallest AI-driven productivity gain. This yields a specific, testable prediction—that
productivity gains attenuate through the hierarchy—which we confirm empirically.3
    Third, a growing literature studies how generative AI reshapes output and consump-
tion on digital platforms. Goldberg and Lam (2026) document a large supply expansion
alongside crowd-out of incumbents on a stock-images marketplace; Reimers and Waldfo-
gel (2026) find that new book releases on Amazon tripled between 2022 and 2025, while
average quality declined; and Zhou and Lee (2024) find that AI adoption raises creators’
output and engagement on an image-sharing platform. We extend this work to software,
documenting an analogous supply expansion across major software marketplaces.
   Finally, our aggregate analysis connects to a longstanding debate about whether and
how quickly productivity gains at the micro level translate into aggregate output growth,
and about the complementary organizational investments required for them to do so
(Solow, 1987; Bresnahan et al., 2002; Bloom et al., 2012; Brynjolfsson et al., 2021). We
provide early evidence on this question for AI in the context of software development.
2Relatedly, Restrepo (2025), Korinek and Suh (2024), and Davidson et al. (2026) study economies approaching
 full automation, asking whether growth or wages remain constrained by what is not automated.
3Our model is also related to hierarchical models of knowledge work, in particular Garicano (2000) and
 Ide and Talamàs (2025), for which Kim and Koning (2026) document evidence that AI-native startups
 have flatter hierarchies than comparable firms. While the hierarchies in these models are in skill—less
 knowledgeable workers handle routine tasks while more knowledgeable workers handle exceptions—we
 consider a hierarchy in the production process, aggregating more granular outputs into higher-level ones.


                                                     6
2     Background
This section outlines a typical developer workflow and the generations of generative AI
coding tools we study.

2.1   Developer Workflow
Software developers write code in integrated development environments (IDEs), such
as Visual Studio Code (VS Code), which provide syntax highlighting, error checking,
and debugging tools. Code is organized in plain-text source files, grouped into projects
and modules. Projects are managed with version-control systems, most commonly Git.
Developers periodically bundle a set of related changes, describe them briefly, and record
the result as a commit, the fundamental, reversible unit of version control.
   Multi-developer projects are coordinated through collaboration platforms such as
GitHub. One core feature of these platforms is an issue tracker (e.g., GitHub Issues),
where developers describe tasks, discuss requirements, and link to relevant code. Once
they agree on a task, a developer is assigned to the issue and implements the changes.
    To work in parallel, developers create separate branches of the codebase, each of which
can be edited independently without immediately incorporating others’ changes. Once
work on an issue is finished, the developer submits a pull request, which indicates that the
changes are ready for review; other developers inspect the code, request modifications, and
ultimately merge it into the project’s main branch. Each project lives in its own repository
(a project-specific folder), and a developer may contribute to several repositories at once.
Finally, for easier distribution, a repository can be packaged into releases—compiled code
associated with a version number and a changelog.

2.2   A Brief History of Generative AI Tools in Software Development
Software development emerged as an early application domain for generative AI due to
the inherently structured nature of computer code and the availability of large training
data. Since 2022, a wide range of generative AI coding tools have become available,
evolving rapidly in scope and autonomy. We distinguish three categories: autocomplete,
synchronous (sync) agents, and asynchronous (async) agents.

Autocomplete GitHub launched GitHub Copilot in June 2022, before ChatGPT, as the
first widely adopted generative AI coding assistant.4 Copilot runs inside the IDE and com-
pletes code as the developer types, generating snippets, comments, and documentation
4While earlier IDE-integrated code-completion tools existed, GitHub Copilot was the first to be widely
 adopted; many others have since followed with similar offerings, such as Codeium (late 2022), Cursor
 (March 2023), and Amazon CodeWhisperer (April 2023).


                                                  7
from the surrounding context.5

Sync Agents Beginning in early 2025, several AI-assisted coding tools evolved to support
an agentic framework in which a developer submits a prompt describing a task, and the
agent works on it directly in the IDE or from the command line. Unlike chatbot-based
workflows, in which developers typically copy and paste suggested code, the agent can
navigate the codebase, propose and apply edits across multiple files, execute code (e.g.,
running unit tests), and iteratively refine its actions in real time. The developer watches
the agent work, accepts or rejects its changes, and is still the one who commits them. Since
the developer supervises the agent as it works, we call these sync agents.

Async Agents A more recent development is async coding agents, which operate au-
tonomously for extended periods with little or no human interaction. Developers assign
a high-level task—a feature, a bug fix, a refactor—and the agent plans and executes it, so
async agents can take on larger and more complex tasks than sync agents.
    In May 2025, GitHub introduced the GitHub Agent, which operates directly within
GitHub. The agent is invoked by assigning it an issue, in the same way as assigning a task
to a human collaborator; see Appendix Figure OA-3(a) for an example. Upon assignment,
the agent launches on a cloud-based virtual machine, autonomously identifies relevant
code, makes edits, runs tests, and pushes commits to a draft pull request. Once the task
is completed, the developer is notified and can review the proposed changes, request
revisions, or merge the pull request.
   OpenAI Codex offers similar async functionality, autonomously modifying code and
opening pull requests.6 Codex differs from the GitHub Agent in how tasks are assigned:
rather than through a GitHub issue, developers assign tasks through an external interface
on OpenAI’s website. Appendix Figure OA-3(b) shows an example task assignment.
    While our classification of AI coding tools into these generations follows the evolution
of the tools, the line between sync and async agents has blurred more recently: most
sync tools today (such as Claude Code) can also operate in async mode, executing tasks
autonomously for extended periods.7 The practical distinction thus rests on the complexity
5Following the launch of ChatGPT in November 2022, developers also quickly adopted AI chatbots for coding-
 related tasks. In September 2023, GitHub Copilot and Cursor integrated chat-based functionality directly
 into IDEs, reducing context switching and giving the chatbot direct access to the codebase. Because chatbot
 usage spans many platforms and is not coding-specific, we do not analyze chatbots as a separate category.
6Several other async agents can be initiated directly on GitHub, including Cursor Agents, Google Jules, and
 Devin, but these collectively account for less than 10% of async agent activity on public repositories and
 are excluded from our analysis. We also exclude “vibe coding” tools such as Replit and Lovable, which are
 primarily targeted at non-developers.
7Claude Code added an async mode in October 2025. Meanwhile, OpenAI released a Codex desktop app
 supporting synchronous use in February 2026.

of the assigned task and the degree of developer supervision rather than on the tool itself.
As a result, some of the sync-agent effect we estimate may partly reflect asynchronous
use by developers we classify as sync adopters. This affects only the attribution of effects
between the sync and async modes, not the total effect of agentic tools.

3     A Model of Software Production
The previous section described how software production proceeds through a hierarchy of
stages—from lines of code to files, commits, pull requests, and releases—and how succes-
sive generations of AI tools intervene at different points in this pipeline. We now formalize
this structure in a model of software production. All proofs are given in Appendix A.

3.1        Setup
Production proceeds through 𝑆 layers, ordered from most granular to most aggregate. At
the lowest layer, the developer writes lines of code. At each subsequent layer 𝑠 ≥ 2, output
of layer 𝑠 − 1 is converted into output of layer 𝑠 using additional input, 𝑒 𝑠 . This process
is illustrated in Figure 2, where lines of code are combined into files, files into commits,
and commits into pull requests. In this process, the additional input is the effort of review,
coordination, and design: checking that a file is correct, deciding which changes belong
together in a commit, and integrating commits into a coherent pull request.

Production at each layer.            At the first layer, where lines of code are produced, output is
given by:

human, the AI, or both, and is formally defined below. At each subsequent layer 𝑠 ≥ 2,

where ℎ 𝑠 = ℎ 𝑠 + ℎ 𝑠𝑟 is the developer’s own effort at layer 𝑠, split between time spent
                                 𝑝
generating output directly (ℎ 𝑠 ) and time spent reviewing AI-generated input (ℎ 𝑠𝑟 ), and
𝑎 𝑠 ≥ 0 is the quantity of AI-generated input at layer 𝑠. Each input has a cost—developer
            𝑝
time for ℎ 𝑠 and ℎ 𝑠𝑟 and inference and prompting costs for 𝑎 𝑠 .
    AI enters effective input in two ways. The first is through 𝐵 𝑠 ≥ 1, which multiplies the
developer’s own effort and therefore captures a direct productivity effect. We normalize
𝐵 𝑠 = 1 in the absence of AI, so that one unit of developer effort produces one unit of
effective input at layer 𝑠. The second is through the Leontief term min(𝑎 𝑠 , 𝜙 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠𝑟 ), where
AI directly creates input at layer 𝑠, but the developer must spend review time ℎ 𝑠𝑟 for that
output to become productive. The parameter 𝜙 𝑠 ≥ 0 therefore indexes the quality of AI
input—the higher is 𝜙 𝑠 , the less review time the developer must spend for each unit of AI
input to become productive. These two parameters, 𝐵 𝑠 and 𝜙 𝑠 , capture distinct channels
through which AI affects production:

Definition 1 (Augmentation). Augmentation arises when 𝐵 𝑠 > 1: the developer’s own effort is
                                 𝑝
amplified through the term 𝐵 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠 . AI makes the human more productive at layer 𝑠—for example, by
suggesting code completions or highlighting potential issues during code review.

Definition 2 (Partial automation). Partial automation arises when 𝜙 𝑠 > 0: AI-generated output
at layer 𝑠 becomes productive with some human review. Automation is only partial because human
input remains essential: without human review, AI input has no value.

Definition 3 (Full automation). In the limit 𝜙 𝑠 → ∞, an arbitrarily small amount of review
suffices: for any ℎ 𝑠𝑟 > 0, min(𝑎 𝑠 , 𝜙 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠𝑟 ) → 𝑎 𝑠 . Automation is full because the human is no longer
needed at layer 𝑠.

                                                       10
3.2    Discussion
Augmentation vs. automation. In the model, augmentation and automation do not
                                       𝑝
coexist within a layer. Because 𝐵 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠 and min(𝑎 𝑠 , 𝜙 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠𝑟 ) enter 𝑒 𝑠 as perfect substitutes in
Equation (3), the developer’s optimal allocation at each layer is a corner solution: she
devotes her effort either to direct production—augmented by 𝐵 𝑠 —or to reviewing AI-
generated input (𝑎 𝑠 ). This is by design: contemporary AI coding tools are typically used
in one mode at a time, not blended within a layer. Therefore, the two channels capture
economically distinct use cases of AI, and ℎ 𝑠𝑟 makes explicit the review and integration
effort that defines how developer work has changed.8

Generality. Our analysis focuses on the specific layers of software production, but the
framework applies more generally. For example, some layers may correspond to creative
and design work—for instance, architectural decisions about how to structure a codebase,
or the initial idea for a feature. These layers are analogous to the “idea production function”
in the growth literature (Aghion et al., 2019; Jones and Tonetti, 2026), and the same logic
applies: AI can generate candidate designs or feature proposals (𝑎 𝑠 > 0), but human
judgment is required to evaluate and select among them (𝜙 𝑠 finite).

Relationship to the production function literature. While we set up the model hierar-
chically, the resulting technology is a nested CES production function (see Appendix A.1
for the derivation). Nested CES is a natural representation for hierarchical production
because it is weakly separable across nests, with each nest homothetic: the inputs of a nest
can be summarized by a single index that enters the nest above (Shephard, 1953; Demirer,
2026). In our setting, this intermediate input is the output of the previous layer, 𝑦 𝑠−1 ,
which is then combined with effective input 𝑒 𝑠 at layer 𝑠.

3.3    Where AI Coding Tools Enter the Production Hierarchy
We now describe how the three generations of AI coding tools we study map to the model’s
parameters—specifically, which layers they augment (𝐵 𝑠 > 1), which layers they automate
(𝑎 𝑠 > 0), and which layers remain purely human. Table 1 summarizes the mapping.

Autocomplete. Autocomplete tools help the developer write code faster in the first layer
by proposing code snippets as they type:

                                               𝑝
                                    𝑒 1 = 𝐵1 ℎ 1 ,      𝐵1 > 1,         𝜙1 = 0.                                  (4)
8If using AI were costless, the augmentation parameter 𝐵 𝑠 and the automation parameter 𝜙 𝑠 would play
                                                                                                 𝑝
 identical roles—each simply multiplies a unit of human effort to deliver effective input (𝐵 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠 versus 𝜙 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠𝑟 ).
 What distinguishes them is the cost of using AI in practice, which has two components: a direct monetary
 cost (inference) and a time cost (prompting, configuration, and review).


                                                         11
               Table 1: Where AI Coding Tools Enter the Production Hierarchy

                        𝑠=1                 𝑠=2             𝑠=3                 𝑠=4           𝑠=5          𝑠=6
 Tool                  (Code)              (Files)        (Commits)             (PRs)       (Projects)   (Releases)
                    Augmenting               —                 —                   —             —          —
 Autocomplete
                      Partial           Partial         Augmenting           Augmenting
 Sync agent         automation        automation                                                 —          —
                    Partial/full      Partial/full        Partial/full       Partial/full

Notes: “Augmenting” denotes layers where AI raises the developer’s productivity (𝐵 𝑠 > 1); “partial automation”
denotes layers where AI generates output subject to human review (𝜙 𝑠 > 0); “—” indicates no AI involvement.

                                                                                             𝑝
The developer still writes every line, but each unit of human effort ℎ1 produces more
output. Since autocomplete does not operate at any higher layer, all effective input above
layer 1, 𝑒 𝑠 , remains purely human (𝜙 𝑠 = 0 so 𝑎 𝑠 = 0 for all 𝑠, and 𝐵 𝑠 = 1 for 𝑠 ≥ 2). The
productivity gain from autocomplete must therefore pass through every subsequent layer
before reaching final output.

Sync agents. Sync agents can autonomously generate code and files, but the developer
monitors the process in real time. This corresponds to AI output generation in layers 1
and 2 (𝜙1 , 𝜙2 > 0), but the developer must review and approve the output:

                                    𝑒 𝑠 = min(𝑎 𝑠 , 𝜙 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠𝑟 ),        𝑠 = 1, 2.                              (5)

At the higher layers, the agent does not generate commits or pull requests directly, but it
can still augment the human input in these layers—for example, by summarizing changes,
documenting code, or highlighting relevant context for review:

                                     𝑝
                          𝑒 𝑠 = 𝐵𝑠 ℎ 𝑠 ,       𝐵 𝑠 > 1,           𝜙 𝑠 = 0,      𝑠 = 3, 4.                     (6)

Therefore, the sync agent operates through both channels: it partially automates layers
1–2 (𝜙 𝑠 > 0) and augments the developer at layers 3–4 (𝐵 𝑠 > 1).

Async agents. Async agents operate more autonomously. Unlike sync agents, they
generate not only code and files but also commits and pull requests directly, without
requiring the developer’s real-time involvement. The agent can generate output at all
layers up to and including pull requests:

                                 𝑒 𝑠 = min(𝑎 𝑠 , 𝜙 𝑠 ℎ 𝑠𝑟 ),        𝑠 = 1, 2, 3, 4.                           (7)




                                                          12
At layers 5 and 6—projects and releases—the agent generates no output, so effective input
there remains purely human (𝜙 𝑠 = 0 and 𝐵 𝑠 = 1 for 𝑠 = 5, 6).

3.4   The Elasticity of Substitution and Shock Propagation
We now explore how an AI-generated productivity gain at a given layer translates into
final output, holding the allocation of human input across layers fixed for simplicity. In
the long run, developers could reoptimize their input across layers in response to AI; we
consider this as an extension in Section 3.5.
    Consider an AI-driven productivity gain only at layer 𝑠−1, through either augmentation
or automation, that raises output there by a factor 𝐴 > 1 with the same human input. The
pass-through to the next layer is:

                                  𝑦˜ 𝑠 (𝐴)
                                           = [𝜃𝑠 𝐴𝜌𝑠 + (1 − 𝜃𝑠 )]1/𝜌𝑠 ,                           (8)
                                      𝑦𝑠

where 𝑦˜ 𝑠 (𝐴) denotes output at layer 𝑠 after the productivity gain at layer 𝑠 − 1. Two
parameters govern this pass-through:
                                                    𝜌𝑠
                          𝜕 ln 𝑦 𝑠           𝛼 𝑠 𝑦 𝑠−1                           𝜎𝑠 − 1
                    𝜃𝑠 ≡           =                            ,         𝜌𝑠 =          .         (9)
                         𝜕 ln 𝑦 𝑠−1 𝛼 𝑠 𝑦 𝜌𝑠 + (1 − 𝛼 𝑠 ) 𝑒 𝑠𝜌𝑠                    𝜎𝑠
                                            𝑠−1

Here 𝜃𝑠 is the output elasticity of 𝑦 𝑠 with respect to upstream input 𝑦 𝑠−1 , capturing how
heavily layer 𝑠 leans on layer-(𝑠 − 1) output rather than on effective input 𝑒 𝑠 . The second
parameter, 𝜌 𝑠 = (𝜎𝑠 − 1)/𝜎𝑠 , reflects how substitutable 𝑦 𝑠−1 and 𝑒 𝑠 are at layer 𝑠. At given
input levels, the pair (𝜃𝑠 , 𝜎𝑠 ) is a reparametrization of (𝛼 𝑠 , 𝜎𝑠 ), since Equation (9) is one-
to-one in 𝛼 𝑠 . We therefore state the comparative statics in (𝜃𝑠 , 𝜎𝑠 ), the parametrization we
take to the data in Section 6.3. Two results follow.

Proposition 1 (Comparative statics of pass-through). Fix a productivity shock 𝐴 > 1 at layer
𝑠 − 1. The pass-through 𝑦˜ 𝑠 (𝐴)/𝑦 𝑠 defined in Equation (8) is:

  (a) strictly increasing in the elasticity of substitution between upstream and effective input at
      layer 𝑠 (𝜎𝑠 );

  (b) strictly increasing in the output elasticity of 𝑦 𝑠 with respect to upstream input (𝜃𝑠 ).

    This proposition shows that the key parameter determining the rate of propagation
is the elasticity of substitution 𝜎𝑠 . When 𝜎𝑠 < 1 (complements), upstream output is a
poor substitute for effective input. A developer who receives double the code but has the
same time to review it cannot produce twice as many files, so only part of the gain passes

                                                   13
 through. In the limit 𝜎𝑠 → 0 the technology approaches Leontief, 𝑦 𝑠 → min(𝑦 𝑠−1 , 𝑒 𝑠 ),
 and the gain vanishes completely once the unchanged human input becomes the binding
 factor. When 𝜎𝑠 > 1 (substitutes), by contrast, an increase in 𝑦 𝑠−1 translates more fully into
 𝑦 𝑠 : because upstream output can stand in for effective input, the fixed review capacity
 does not hold the gain back. The proposition also shows that pass-through is increasing
 in 𝜃𝑠 : the more layer 𝑠 relies on upstream output rather than its own effective input, the
 more of the gain it inherits.
    We next present two corollaries that show how a productivity gain from AI at one layer
 translates into changes in output further along the hierarchy.

 Corollary 1 (Attenuation and layer of entry). (i) A productivity shock at layer 𝑘 attenuates
      as it propagates through the hierarchy: the effect on output at layer 𝑠 > 𝑘 is strictly smaller
      than the effect at layer 𝑘, and the cumulative effect at layer 𝑠 is the composition of pass-throughs
      at each intervening layer.9

   (ii) Consider two technologies that each raise output by the same factor 𝐴 > 1, but one enters at
        layer 𝑘 and the other at layer 𝑘 ′ > 𝑘 (closer to final output). The effect on final output from
        the technology entering at layer 𝑘 ′ is strictly larger, because the shock passes through fewer
        layers of attenuation.

     The intuition is straightforward: at each layer, the upstream output must be combined
 with effective input whose productivity has not changed, so only a fraction of the gain
 passes through. The earlier the layer at which a shock enters, the more such bottlenecks
 it must traverse, and the more the gain is compressed.10 It follows that the impact of any
 AI tool depends not only on its effectiveness but also on where it enters the production
 hierarchy. A tool that provides a modest improvement at a high layer may increase final
 output by more than a tool that provides a large improvement at a low layer. Autocomplete,
 for example, enters at layer 1, so its productivity gain is attenuated five times before
 reaching releases; agents enter as high as layer 4 and are attenuated only twice.

 Proposition 2 (Shape of attenuation). The shape of pass-through depends on whether upstream
 and effective input are complements or substitutes:

   (a) Shape of attenuation across layers. Assume 𝜎 and 𝜃 are common across layers, and let
       𝐺 𝑠 ≡ 𝑦˜ 𝑠 /𝑦 𝑠 denote the cumulative output gain at layer 𝑠 following a shock 𝐴 at the origin
 9This result requires that upstream and effective input both have positive weight at every layer (𝜃𝑠 ∈ (0, 1)).
10We maintain throughout that units at each layer are comparable before and after AI adoption. If AI packed
  more value into each unit, quantities would understate output, which would create spurious attenuation. We
  think this is unlikely, as generating the attenuation pattern would require value per unit to rise progressively
  across layers; we further test this empirically in Section 6.2.


                                                        14
        layer. Let the survival rate 𝑅 𝑠 ≡ ln 𝐺 𝑠+1 /ln 𝐺 𝑠 ∈ (0, 1) be the share of the gain that survives
        one more layer. Then 𝑅 𝑠 is strictly increasing in 𝑠 when 𝜎 < 1 (complements), constant at 𝜃
        when 𝜎 = 1 (Cobb–Douglas), and strictly decreasing in 𝑠 when 𝜎 > 1 (substitutes).

   (b) Unbounded upstream input. When the upstream productivity gain grows without bound
       (𝐴 → ∞), as under full automation of layer 1, the output gain converges to:

                                          𝜎
                   𝑦˜      (1 − 𝜃) 𝜎−1 < ∞          if 𝜎 < 1 (complements),
                              
                          
                        =                                                                               (10)
                   𝑦 𝐴→∞ ∞
                                                    if 𝜎 ≥ 1 (Cobb–Douglas or substitutes).

     Result (a) shows how the productivity gain is absorbed as it propagates through the
 hierarchy. The special case of Cobb–Douglas (𝜎𝑠 = 1) provides a useful benchmark: each
 layer preserves a constant fraction 𝜃 of the previous layer’s log gain (𝑅 𝑠 = 𝜃 for all 𝑠), so
 ln 𝐺 𝑠 decays geometrically. With complements (𝜎 < 1), 𝑅 𝑠 rises with 𝑠: the first layer above
 the shock absorbs a disproportionate share of the attenuation, and subsequent layers eat
 into the remaining gain much less. With substitutes (𝜎 > 1), 𝑅 𝑠 falls with 𝑠: early layers
 preserve most of the gain, so a larger share reaches deeper layers, even though later layers
 attenuate more aggressively in relative terms.
     Result (b) shows that with complements, even unbounded productivity gains in an
 upstream layer yield bounded gains. The reason is that with complements every input
 is essential, so even infinite AI input generates finite output if the human input remains
 finite. This result connects directly to the “weak links” framework in the macroeconomic
 growth literature (Kremer, 1993; Jones, 2011; Aghion et al., 2019; Jones and Tonetti, 2026).
 In that literature, when tasks in the economy are complements, automating some tasks
 has bounded effects on aggregate output—total production is constrained by the weakest
 link.11 Our model applies this logic vertically: the “tasks” are the layers of the production
 hierarchy, and the weak link is the human-bottlenecked layer with the least AI capability.
     The propagation results also relate to the literature on the pass-through of productiv-
 ity shocks in production networks. In our setting, the hierarchical structure introduces
 nonlinearity: the impact of a shock at a given layer depends on input levels at that and
 higher layers, and on the elasticity of substitution between them. This connects to Baqaee
 and Farhi (2019), who extend Hulten’s theorem (Hulten, 1978) to a nonlinear environment
 in which substitution elasticities shape how shocks propagate through the network.
11Jones (2026) shows that fully automating a subset of tasks raises output by at most a factor determined by
  the spending share of the tasks that are not automated; our bound (10) coincides with this result, with the
  share parameter 𝜃 in the role of Jones’s spending share (see Appendix A.6).




                                                     15
3.5   Alternative Models and Extensions
So far, we have intentionally kept the model stylized to present the role of AI tools and to
emphasize the most widely considered hypothesis linking task-level output to final output:
complementarities and the weak-link hypothesis. However, there are alternative models
of the software development production function that can generate similar attenuation
patterns. We develop these alternatives in Appendix B, derive the corresponding pass-
through expressions, and discuss how they differ from our baseline. In Section 6.3 we test
the fit of these models to our empirical results.
    One such model replaces complementarity with decreasing returns, as in a Cobb–
                            𝛾𝑠 𝛿 𝑠
Douglas technology 𝑦 𝑠 = 𝑦 𝑠−1 𝑒 𝑠 with 𝛾𝑠 + 𝛿 𝑠 < 1. There, an overall productivity gain of
𝐴 at a layer passes through as 𝐴 𝛾𝑠 , so gains attenuate as they travel up the hierarchy even
in the absence of complementarities, and decreasing returns deepen this attenuation. A
second model combines complementarity with decreasing returns to scale, in which case
attenuation is amplified because both channels operate at once.
    A third model relaxes the assumption that developers hold their inputs fixed and
instead allows them to reallocate inputs across layers. In this extension attenuation still
arises, but is weaker than without input reallocation, since inputs are reallocated toward the
layers with smaller AI productivity gains, which raises output relative to a fixed allocation.
Reallocation can therefore be seen as a way of easing the bottlenecks, and it corresponds
to the widely documented reorganization of production in response to new technologies
(Brynjolfsson and Hitt, 2000; Bresnahan et al., 2002). While one can expect this to happen
in the medium run, one could go further and consider changing the production process
entirely to relieve the bottlenecks through complementary investments (Bresnahan and
Trajtenberg, 1995; Brynjolfsson et al., 2021; McElheran et al., 2025). Since such changes
have been shown to take a long time, we do not consider them as an extension.

4     Data and Summary Statistics
This section describes our data sources, how we identify adopters of each AI tool, the
selection of the developer control pools, additional sample restrictions, and summary
statistics.

4.1   Data Sources
Our analysis draws on three data sources: GitHub data on activity in public reposito-
ries, internal Microsoft data on the adoption and usage of AI coding tools available in
GitHub Copilot, and data on four major software marketplaces. Detailed data collection
procedures are provided in Appendix C.

                                             16
 GitHub Public Repository Data GitHub is the dominant platform for hosting and col-
 laborating on software projects, with over 256 million developers and 517 million public
 repositories.12 Repositories can be either public or private; our data include only activity
 on public repositories. While this restriction misses closed-source activity, open-source
 software is large and economically important in its own right (Wright et al., 2023; Hoffmann
 et al., 2024; Gortmaker, 2026). We later use our limited data on AI activity in closed-source
 repositories to assess the external validity of our public-activity results.
     GitHub records detailed developer activity data—including commits, pull requests,
 and issues. We use these data first to identify the adoption of some of the AI tools we study
 (Claude Code and the async agents) from the traces these tools leave in public repositories.
 For these developers, we use the GitHub REST API to download complete commit and
 pull request histories from January 2020 through May 2026: 388 million commits and
 46.9 million pull requests.13 For each commit, we retrieve the author, timestamp, commit
 message, lines added and deleted, and the files changed. For each pull request, we retrieve
 the creator, creation date, requested reviewers, assignees, and branch name. These data
 allow us to construct a range of outcome measures, such as lines of code added, number of
 repositories touched, and files changed, which we aggregate into a weekly activity panel
 for all developers in our sample.14

 Microsoft Data We use confidential data from Microsoft on GitHub Copilot usage. These
 data report, for all GitHub Copilot subscribers, the number of requests made to each tool
 in the Copilot subscription—autocomplete, the sync agent, and the async agent—between
 April and December 2025, together with subscription start dates from 2022. We use these
 data to construct adoption events for GitHub Autocomplete and GitHub Sync, and to
 validate our public adoption measures for the async agents.

 Software Marketplace Data We obtain panel data from March 2020 through August 2026
 on four software marketplaces: the Apple App Store, the Google Play Store, the Chrome
 Web Store, which we license through the APIs of SimilarWeb and chrome-stats.com, and
 SourceForge, which we scrape directly from the site. These data provide a time series of
 the apps available on each platform, together with app-level measures of usage such as
12As of August 2026, based on our own calculations described in Appendix C.
13An alternative data source is GHArchive, which is commonly used in the previous literature (Kreitmeir and
  Raschky, 2024; Gortmaker, 2026). Its advantage is that the data are available for bulk download and are not
  subject to GitHub API rate limits. However, our investigation of GHArchive revealed data quality issues
  after early 2025—including missing dates and incomplete commit activity coverage—making it unreliable
  for our paper. We therefore opt to download the data directly through the GitHub REST API.
14Because we obtain the data through API calls, our sample does not include coding activity that was later
  deleted by users. In addition, we collect activity information only from the main branches of the repositories
  to which developers contribute. However, in Section 6.2 we also analyze branch creation and deletion to
  capture activity on off-main branches.

tool became publicly available; we include adoptions in the calendar year of launch. “Copilot subscriptions”
and “Microsoft internal usage” are confidential data provided by Microsoft; “Public GitHub” data are collected

on a public repository.


installs, downloads, user counts, and ratings. Appendix C.7 provides the full details on
data sources, collection method, and variables for each marketplace.

4.2     Construction of Adoption Events
Measuring AI adoption across different tools is challenging because usage data are typically
proprietary. We observe internal telemetry for GitHub Copilot, but no equivalent exists for
the other tools we study. We therefore combine these confidential records with publicly
observable GitHub activity to identify adoption events across multiple tools and vendors.
Table 2 summarizes the resulting adoption rules; we describe each in turn below.

GitHub Autocomplete We identify GitHub Autocomplete users using GitHub Copilot
subscription data from 2022. Since autocomplete was the only AI tool available under the
Copilot subscription at that time, we treat the subscription start date as the adoption date
for GitHub Autocomplete.

Sync Agents For sync agents, we identify two tools: GitHub Sync and Claude Code.
GitHub Sync adoption comes from internal Microsoft data: we use the first date each user
invokes GitHub Copilot in agent mode as the adoption date. We identify Claude Code
adopters by searching for CLAUDE.md files in public repositories. Claude automatically
reads these configuration files when starting a conversation, making their presence a
reliable indicator of Claude Code usage. For each user who has authored a commit to
a CLAUDE.md file, we define the adoption date as the date of their earliest such commit.
Figure 3(a) plots the resulting Claude Code adoption events, which ramp up sharply
around the release of Opus 4 in late May 2025 and continue throughout our sample.

 Notes: Each panel plots the weekly count of new adopters for the corresponding tool over our sample window.
 Vertical dashed red lines mark the release dates of frontier models behind each tool: Anthropic Opus 4 (May 22,
 2025), Opus 4.1 (Aug 5, 2025), and Opus 4.5 (Nov 24, 2025) in Panel 3(a); OpenAI GPT-5 (Aug 7, 2025), GPT-5.1
 (Nov 13, 2025), and GPT-5.2 (Dec 11, 2025), together with the date Codex agents were given internet access (Jun 3,
 2025), in Panel 3(b). Panel 3(c) omits such markers as the underlying model behind GitHub Agent has not been
 publicly versioned during our window. We omit the corresponding distributions for GitHub Autocomplete and
 GitHub Sync as those data are considered sensitive.


 Async Agents Unlike sync agents, async agents interact directly with the version-control
 system and hence leave unambiguous traces. Using data from GitHub repositories, we
 retrieve all pull requests created by the Copilot coding agent bot for the GitHub Agent,
 and all pull requests opened on Codex-created branches for Codex.15 From these pull
 requests, we identify the associated human users through co-author information in commit
 messages, requested reviewers, creators, or assignees, and use the earliest such pull request
 as their adoption date. Figures 3(b) and 3(c) plot the resulting adoption events for the
 two async tools. While Codex adoption jumps sharply with the introduction of internet
 access in early June 2025 and remains roughly stable thereafter, GitHub Agent adoption is
 concentrated noticeably later in the sample, accelerating through the late summer and fall.
 This timing gap suggests that the two adopter pools may differ, so cross-tool differences
 in treatment effects could partly reflect who adopts rather than the tools themselves.
                      Async agents come bundled with sync capabilities: a developer with access to the
15The GitHub Agent’s pull requests are authored by the bot account copilot-swe-agent[bot]; Codex pull
  requests are opened on branches with the prefix codex/.



                                                                                                 19
 GitHub Agent or Codex can also use the same product interactively, and Microsoft internal
 data show that async adoption is associated with roughly a fivefold increase in sync usage,
 with only a small share of developers using the async agent exclusively. We therefore treat
 async adoption as the joint adoption of sync and async capabilities, and we separate the
 two effects in our empirical analysis: agent-authored commits can come only from the
 async agent, while changes in the developer’s own commits reflect sync use. Accordingly,
 in our per-tool estimates, the GitHub Agent adopters’ human-authored activity enters the
 GitHub Sync sample, since both reflect use of the same product’s sync mode.

 4.3   Selecting Adopters and Summary Statistics
 We impose three restrictions on the adopters we study. First, we include only adoptions
 in the calendar year of the tool’s release: for autocomplete, this rules out contamination
 from tools that arrived later, such as chatbots, and for the tools launched in 2025, it ensures
 that controls are drawn from a year before agentic tools were widely adopted.16 Second,
 we require that we can retrieve an adopter’s commit history: this drops accounts that
 have been deleted or suspended, accounts at bot scale (more than 100,000 commits in
 the window or 1,000 in a day), and adopters with no public commits, and reduces the
 sample from 752,917 to 669,107 adopters. Third, we require at least one week with positive
 commits at or before week −11 relative to adoption, when our event study begins, so that
 we study developers who were already coding on GitHub rather than accounts created
 to try out a tool; this leaves 521,329 adopters. Appendix Table OA-4 reports how many
 adopters remain after each of these restrictions, tool by tool.
     Table 3 reports pre-adoption summary statistics for adopters after the sample restric-
 tions: weekly averages over the eleven weeks before adoption for the six outcomes span-
 ning the production hierarchy. We highlight three features. First, activity is highly dis-
 persed within each sample: for commits, the standard deviation is about 4 times the mean,
 and the 5th percentile is zero, reflecting a mix of full-time developers and sporadic contrib-
 utors. This dispersion is why we normalize outcomes by each developer’s own pre-period
 activity in our event study, as described in Section 5.2. Second, autocomplete adopters are
 less active on GitHub than agent adopters, averaging 1.9 commits per week against 3.5 for
 the sync sample. This may partly reflect lower overall activity on GitHub in the earlier
 period, and partly that some GitHub Copilot subscribers are not active on GitHub, coding
 mostly elsewhere. Finally, releases are rare in any given week; we return to this when
 interpreting attenuation across the production hierarchy.

4.4        Construction of the Control Pool
We also collect data on a broad set of GitHub developers from which we later draw matched
controls. Downloading commit histories for every GitHub account is infeasible under API
rate limits, so we use GHArchive, a public log of all events on public repositories, to
screen for active accounts. We include every account with at least twenty push events over
2021–2025 (13.4 million accounts) together with a stratified random sample of 10 million
less active accounts. Of these, 20.8 million accounts still exist and have at least one public
commit; these developers form the control pool, and we download their complete commit
and pull request lists—2.7 billion commits and 201 million pull requests.
   We collect commit details, which requires one API call per commit, only for the devel-
opers matched to an adopter in Section 5.1 (344 million commit records), from which we
construct the same outcome variables as for the treated group. Appendix C.3 describes
the construction in detail and Appendix Table OA-5 reports the counts at each stage.

5     Productivity Effects Across Generations of Tools
Our empirical analysis examines the productivity effects of AI coding tools along two
dimensions: across successive generations of tools, and across stages of the software
production hierarchy. We organize the analysis as follows. We first describe how we
match adopters to controls and set out our event-study specification. We then use weekly

                                                      21
 commits, our primary task-level productivity measure, to estimate the productivity effects
 across the three generations of tools. Then, in Section 6, we move beyond commits and
 examine outcomes at each layer of the production hierarchy. We interpret these results
 through the lens of our production model to understand how large task-level productivity
 effects translate into gains in final output, and to explore the underlying mechanisms.

 5.1    Matching Strategy
 For each coding tool, we perform a matched event study, pairing each adopter with a
 developer from the control pool who never adopts the tool. We match on pre-adoption
 activity because adoption is not random in time. Developers adopt a new tool when
 they are actively coding, so adopters are, on average, in the middle of an activity spell at
 adoption, and a developer who is unusually active this week tends to remain unusually
 active for some weeks afterward. Comparing adopters to controls drawn at an arbitrary
 time would therefore attribute the tail of the spell to the tool. Matching on activity at and
 before adoption is how we match on the trajectory that follows.
     Concretely, each adopter is matched to a control who (i) made at least one commit
 and pull request in the pseudo-adoption week—the strongest activity signal available to
 us—and (ii) is closest in total commits over the eleven preceding weeks17, one-to-one and
 without replacement.18 Because there is judgment in choosing the activity signal, we also
 re-estimate the event study under weaker activity requirements—only a commit, or only
 a pull request, in the (pseudo-)adoption week—and obtain similar estimates (Appendix
 Table OA-11). We also conduct placebo tests using non-AI tools, discussed in Section 5.5.
     Table 4 compares the resulting treated and matched control units for each event study.
 Because our donor pool covers developers at any given activity level, matching on pre-
 period commits ensures that median commit activity is identical across the two groups.
 Matching on commits also brings the other pre-period outcomes close, though not per-
 fectly: treated developers change more lines per week, while control developers open
 more pull requests and touch more repositories. Where the two groups differ, controls
 are if anything the more active—the intended consequence of requiring controls to have
 both a pull request and a commit in the pseudo-adoption week. Because our specification
 includes a matched-pair fixed effect, these level differences do not enter the estimates;
 what matters is that pre-trends are flat, which we verify for every outcome below.
17As we match on total commits and not the week-by-week pattern, pre-trends remain informative.
18For tools whose adoption we observe on GitHub (Claude Code, the async agents, and Docker), the adoption
  event is itself public activity. For tools observed through Microsoft subscription or usage records (autocom-
  plete, GitHub Sync, and GitHub Pro), which need not coincide with public activity, we impose the same
  commit-plus-pull-request requirement on adopters, so that adopters and controls are selected on the same
  signal; estimates are similar when this requirement is imposed on all adopters (Appendix Table OA-11).

Notes: This table presents median features of treated units and the control units matched to them, separately for
each productivity event study we conduct. For each outcome variable, “# Treated Units” reports the number
of treated users entering that regression, after dropping developers with no pre-period activity in that outcome
and the top 1% of developers by pre-period volume. “Treated Group” and “Matched Control Group” report the
median of user-level average pre-period outcome values (weeks −11 to −1 relative to the event). For the “Donor
Pool” row under Commits, we report the median of user-level average weekly commits for the full calendar
window of each event study. “GitHub Sync” covers the adopters identified from Microsoft usage data; the GitHub
Sync event studies of Tables 5 and 6 additionally pool the human-authored activity of the GitHub Agent adopters
shown in their own column.


   When matching control users to treated users, we take the control group’s outcomes
from exactly one year earlier, because contemporaneous non-adopters are not a clean
comparison group, for two reasons. First, and most importantly, many developers use AI
tools without leaving a trace in public repositories, so a contemporaneous control group is
contaminated by treatment. Second, in a period of widespread adoption, non-adoption is


                                                       23
 itself informative about a developer’s future: those who have adopted no AI tool by 2025
 are disproportionately likely to be reducing their activity. Contemporaneous non-adopters
 may therefore be on a declining trajectory relative to adopters, a violation of parallel trends
 that matching on pre-adoption activity does not address. Drawing controls from the year
 before the tool was widely available avoids both issues: as no developer in the control pool
 could have adopted, the pool is neither contaminated nor selected on the decision not to
 adopt. Nevertheless, we re-estimate the event study using a contemporaneously matched
 control group as a robustness check in Appendix E.
     Appendix F formally develops the identification argument, showing that the primary
 new identifying assumption required for this strategy (relative to classic difference-in-
 differences) is the absence of calendar-time (aggregate) shocks after the last adoption
 event. Appendix D provides the full details of the matching procedure.

 5.2    Empirical Specification
 Using this sample, we estimate a generalized difference-in-differences specification:

                                             30
                                             Õ
                          𝑌𝑖𝑡𝑇 − 𝑌𝑖𝑡𝐶 =                𝛽 𝑘 · 1{𝑡 − 𝑡 𝑖∗ = 𝑘} + 𝛼 𝑖 + 𝜀𝑖𝑡 ,             (11)
                                          𝑘=−11;𝑘≠−1


 where 𝑌𝑖𝑡𝑇 is the weekly outcome of treated developer 𝑖 in week 𝑡, normalized by her pre-
 period mean19; 𝑌𝑖𝑡𝐶 is the analogous outcome for the matched control; 𝑡 𝑖∗ is the (pseudo-
 )adoption week; and 𝛼 𝑖 is a matched-pair fixed effect. After estimation, we re-normalize
 the pre-period coefficients to have zero mean, so each 𝛽 𝑘 is interpreted as the deviation
 from the average pre-period level. The coefficients 𝛽 𝑘 thus trace the dynamic treatment
 effect at event time 𝑘, expressed as proportional changes relative to the pre-period baseline.
 Standard errors are clustered at the developer level (developer-by-tool when pooling tools).
 We estimate this specification for several outcome measures, including lines of code, files,
 commits, pull requests, repositories, and releases; in all specifications, we winsorize the
 dependent variable (the treated–control difference of normalized outcomes) at the 1st and
 99th percentiles. Appendix D gives the full specification, including pre- and post-window
 indicators.
       Because outcomes are normalized by each developer’s pre-period mean, 𝛽 𝑘 is the
19Dividing by the pre-period mean requires the developer to be active in the estimation outcome between
  weeks −11 and −1. For most outcomes this imposes little additional restriction, since we already condition
  on developers who are active on GitHub (Section 4.3). However, some outcomes, such as releases, are quite
  sparse; for these outcomes we drop developers with no pre-period activity of that type. While this creates
  some differences in the estimation sample across outcomes, we conduct a robustness check restricting the
  sample to developers with pre-period activity in every outcome (Appendix E.2).


                                                         24
 average of individual percentage changes, as with a log outcome. This differs from a level
 effect divided by mean productivity when effects vary with baseline activity, as they do
 here: if a developer with 1 commit per week rises to 2 and one with 100 rises to 110, the
 average percentage change is 55%, while the average level change (5.5 commits) is about
 11% of the overall mean.20 We prefer this to logs, which would require dropping the many
 developers with zero commits in some week, and present it as our main specification,
 leaving level estimation as a robustness check.
     Our main estimates pool AI tools within each generation, following the decomposition
 of Section 4.2: the async-agent effect is estimated from async adopters’ agent-authored
 activity, and the sync-agent effect from their human-authored activity pooled with that
 of the sync-agent adopters. For agent-authored commits, the control’s outcome and the
 adopter’s pre-period outcome are zero by definition, so the same specification reduces to an
 interrupted time series; because the outcome’s own pre-period mean is zero, we normalize
 by the developer’s pre-period mean of total commits. We report product-specific effects in
 a subsequent heterogeneity analysis.

 5.3   Results: Task-Level Productivity
 We now present results on how task-level productivity differs across generations of AI
 tools. Unlike settings such as essay writing or customer service, where output can be scored
 directly, there is no consensus measure of software-development productivity (Petersen,
 2011; Meyer et al., 2014; Ko, 2019; Forsgren et al., 2021). Given this, we follow the AI
 productivity literature in settings such as coding (Cui et al., 2026) and customer service
 (Brynjolfsson et al., 2025) and estimate task-level productivity: holding the unit of work
 roughly fixed, we ask how much more code a developer produces. We use weekly commits
 as our outcome. Commits are a natural discrete unit of coding work, are widely tracked as
 a developer-activity measure in the software-engineering literature (Forsgren et al., 2021;
 DORA, 2024), and are observed at high frequency in our data. In Section 6, we examine
 other outcome variables to understand how AI affects different stages of the production
 process and final output.
     Figure 4 presents the event studies; Table 5 reports the corresponding estimates pooled
 into weeks 1–10, 11–20, and 21–30 after adoption. The effect is shown separately for each
 generation: autocomplete, sync agents, and async agents. The top row shows users who
 adopted GitHub Copilot in 2022, when only AI-based code completion was available. Our
20The pre-period mean is estimated from 11 weeks and is therefore noisy; by Jensen’s inequality, dividing by
  a noisy mean may bias the normalized outcome upward. In practice this bias is small: we estimate a level
  specification, which does not divide by the pre-period mean (Table OA-9), and another specification where
  we calculate the pre-period mean from 52 weeks, and obtain similar results (Appendix Table OA-12).

                                                                            26
estimate in Figure 4(a) shows a 30% increase in the number of commits. The pre-trend is
almost perfectly flat, supporting the parallel trends assumption. As shown in Figure 4(b),
the effect is larger for less active developers, though it remains substantial throughout
the activity distribution (from 20% for the most active to 61% for the least active). Our
estimates are consistent with prior field-experimental estimates of a 26% (SE: 10%) task-
level productivity gain from GitHub Copilot autocomplete (Cui et al., 2026), validating
our design against experimental evidence.
    Moving on to sync agents in the middle row, we again find flat pre-trends and a
sizable effect immediately upon adoption (Figure 4(c)). While the number of commits
temporarily increases sharply—likely due to an experimentation phase—the effect rapidly
decays before rising again to an eventual 150% productivity gain—about 5 times the effect
of autocomplete. The heterogeneity pattern (Figure 4(d)) mirrors that of autocomplete:
effects are largest for less active developers and decrease monotonically as developers
become more active, though even for the most active developers the effect remains around
53%. We caution that, while these heterogeneity results are consistent with the prior
experimental literature (Peng et al., 2023; Brynjolfsson et al., 2025; Cui et al., 2026), there
are theoretical reasons to expect the direction of the heterogeneity to flip as tools improve
(Benzell and Myers, 2026).
    Finally, the bottom row shows the effect of adopting async agents on agent-written
commits (Figure 4(e)). Agent-authored commits are zero by definition before adoption,
and they rise to about 60% of a developer’s baseline commits after adoption. As with the
other tools, effects spike initially before settling at moderate but significant levels, again
larger for less active developers (Figure 4(f)).
    In Table 5, we break out the sync and async effects by product; the corresponding per-
tool event studies are in Appendix Figure OA-4. We see large and statistically significant
effects across all coding tools, suggesting that our results are not driven by the selection of
a particular tool. However, the magnitudes vary by tool: the long-run sync effect is 296%
for Claude Code, 126% for GitHub Sync, and 119% for OpenAI Codex. These differences
can reflect either differential productivity effects of the tools themselves or differences in
the population of developers who adopt each tool. For example, Claude Code’s notably
larger sync effect may reflect its adopters: the tool runs from the command line and was
adopted early in our sample (Figure 3), both markers of sophisticated developers who
might benefit more from any tool. Our empirical strategy cannot distinguish between
these mechanisms.
    Taken together, our results establish that generative AI coding tools have substantial
effects on developer task-level productivity, with effects that grow across successive gener-

ations. Autocomplete increases commits by roughly 30%; the cumulative effect including
sync agents rises to approximately 180%; and additional use of async agents raises it to
240% through agent-authored commits. These effects are positive and statistically signif-
icant for every tool, and persist over at least 30 weeks. The significantly smaller effects of
autocomplete relative to agents are consistent with the nature of these tools as discussed in
Section 3: autocomplete simply augments the developer’s own input when writing code,
whereas agents can lead to partial or full automation.

5.4   Results: Dynamic Treatment Effects
The treatment effects in Figure 4(c) showed a clear increase over event time, with effects
in weeks 21–30 noticeably larger than in earlier weeks. This pattern admits two interpre-
tations: a genuinely dynamic treatment effect that accumulates with time since adoption
(e.g., as users invest in complementary capital or learn to use the tool more effectively), or
a calendar-time effect that operates on all users at each point in time (e.g., as the frontier
models powering these tools improve). The two are difficult to disentangle in the pooled
sample, where adoption dates are spread across calendar time. To make progress, we
restrict attention to the subset of developers who adopted within the first three weeks of
