                         (a) GitHub Autocomplete                                           (b) Claude Code
 Notes: Each panel presents the matched event-study estimate of the effect of adopting the corresponding tool
 on weekly commits, restricted to the subset of developers who adopted in the first three weeks after the tool’s
 release. The empirical specification is otherwise identical to the matched event study in Figure 4(c). Panel 5(a)
 shows GitHub Autocomplete adopters in mid-2022; to our knowledge, the underlying model was not updated
 until February 2023 and was not significantly updated until November 2023 (when it was upgraded to GPT-4),
 so the entire window in this panel reflects a single model generation. Panel 5(b) shows Claude Code adopters in
 mid-2025; the cohort is anchored on the date Claude Code became generally available (May 22, 2025, coinciding
 with the Opus 4 launch) rather than the February 2025 research preview. The shaded regions mark the eras
 during which successive Anthropic Opus models (Opus 4, Opus 4.1, Opus 4.5) were the frontier release, with era
 boundaries corresponding to each model’s public release date.


 each tool’s release. Within this fixed cohort, event time maps directly onto calendar time,
 so we can read calendar-time effects off the horizontal axis. Figure 5 reports the results.
     Panel 5(b) shows that the increasing event-time pattern for Claude Code is preserved
 within the early-adopter cohort, and aligns with the release of successive Opus models:
 the effect declines and stabilizes through the Opus 4 and Opus 4.1 eras, then rises sharply
 once Opus 4.5 becomes the frontier model. Panel 5(a) reinforces this reading by contrast:
 GitHub Autocomplete showed no increasing pattern in event time (Figure 4(a)), and the
 early adopters likewise show no increase in calendar time after the initial spike. This is
 consistent with the fact that the cohort adopted in mid-2022, before any non-trivial update
 to the underlying model—the first came in February 2023, with a more substantial upgrade
 to GPT-4 in November 2023, well outside our window. Together, these patterns suggest
 that the increasing long-run effects we estimate for sync agents are driven primarily by
 improvements in the frontier models that power them, rather than by within-developer
 learning over time.21

 5.5                Robustness Checks
 Our analysis relies on observational data, which raises concerns about identifying a causal
 effect. We summarize several robustness checks here that support a causal interpretation
 of our estimates; full details are in Appendix E.
21Consistent with this interpretation, Sarkar and Melas-Kyriazi (2026) document a 44% increase in weekly agent
  messages on Cursor following the late-2025 releases of Claude Opus 4.5 and GPT-5.2, with the response
  concentrated in more complex tasks.


                                                                29
 Commit quality and size. One concern is that the increase in commits may not reflect
 an increase in task-level output. This could happen for two main reasons. First, AI may
 reduce the quality of commits: the additional commits may consist of buggy code and
 subsequent fixes, reflecting rework rather than new output. Second, the size of commits
 may shrink: if AI leads developers to split a given amount of work into more, smaller
 commits, commit counts would rise without an increase in underlying output. We test the
 first hypothesis by classifying commits into bug fixes and feature work based on commit
 messages, and the second by re-estimating our event study with lines per commit and files
 per commit as outcomes. The share of bug-fix commits relative to feature commits remains
 stable after adoption (Appendix Figure OA-16), and we find no evidence of a decline in
 commit size (Table 7). Together, these results indicate that the increase in commits reflects
 a real increase in task-level output.

 Activity bias. A second concern is that our estimates capture activity bias—periods of
 heightened engagement that coincide with adoption—rather than a true productivity
 effect. Two pieces of evidence argue against this. First, we examine two non-AI coding
 tools: GitHub Pro, a paid subscription without AI features, and Docker, a containerization
 tool that may itself raise productivity and so serves as an upper bound on activity bias.22
 Figure 6 shows that both generate large short-run spikes that decay rapidly, with GitHub
 Pro converging to no long-run effect and Docker stabilizing around 7%, against 153% for
 AI coding tools. Second, if our estimates were driven by activity bias, they would dissipate
 over time, as they do in the placebos within roughly 10 weeks. Instead, Table 5 shows that
 the AI treatment effects remain stable over the 30-week post-adoption window.

 Robustness to empirical specification. To assess the robustness of our specification that
 scales outcomes by the pre-period mean, we also estimate the event study in levels. Specif-
 ically, we estimate a separate level regression for each pre-period-mean decile—allowing
 the effect to differ across baseline-activity groups—and then average the decile-specific es-
 timates. The results in Table OA-9 show that this alternative approach yields effects closely
 matching our pooled normalized estimates. We further examine robustness to other spec-
 ification choices, such as the winsorization threshold and the sample restrictions, and find
 similar results in Appendix Table OA-7 and Appendix Figures OA-12 and OA-13.

 Robustness to matching. To assess how our results depend on the matching procedure,
 we re-estimate the event study under alternative activity requirements. We require only a

  Docker adoption is identified from a developer’s first commit to a Dockerfile, in the same way as Claude

  have sufficient statistical power.

 control; we require both of every adopter, including those whose adoption we observe on
 GitHub; and we additionally require matched pairs to have created their accounts in the
 same year. Appendix Table OA-11 and Appendix Figure OA-11 report the results, which
 are similar to our baseline estimates.23 We also re-match on longer pre-adoption windows,
 using total commits over the 22, 32, 42, or 52 weeks preceding adoption instead of 11, and
 again find similar estimates (Appendix Table OA-12).24

 Using the previous year as controls. As explained in Section 5.1, we match control
 developers to their activity one year before adoption, since contemporaneous controls are
 likely to be using AI tools in ways we do not observe. We nevertheless re-estimate the event
 study with contemporaneous matching (Appendix Table OA-11 and Appendix Figure OA-
 11, Panels OA-11(a) and OA-11(b)). As discussed earlier, this specification yields smaller
 estimates, but only by 21% on average, so even the contemporaneous specification implies
 statistically and economically significant productivity effects of AI. We also provide a
 formal discussion in Appendix F of the conditions under which this design identifies the
 treatment effect, and argue that these conditions are likely to hold in our setting.
23We run these variants for commits and pull requests only, since downloading the remaining outcomes for
  the full control pool is prohibitively time- and compute-intensive. We do not condition on account creation
  year in the main specification because doing so lowers the matching rate.
24We nevertheless prefer the eleven-week window: activity comes in spells, and commits over the past year
  are a weaker signal of whether a developer is currently in an active spell than commits over the past quarter.




                                                                           31
External validity: public vs. private repositories. Our adoption measure relies on pub-
licly observable activity, raising the concern that we miss developers who use AI tools
exclusively on private repositories. We provide two pieces of evidence that our sample
nevertheless captures a large share of total activity on GitHub. First, using internal GitHub
data on async agent usage in November 2025, we find that our public-repository measure
identifies 24.2% of all async agent users and captures 72.9% of these adopters’ usage activ-
ity. Second, using GitHub repository identifiers, we estimate that 38% of all repositories
ever created are public. Evidence from proprietary enterprise data also points in the same
direction as our public-repository results (Chen and Stratton, 2026).

Robustness to activity deletion. Because we collect our data through the GitHub API,
activity that developers have since deleted does not appear in our sample. To gauge how
much this matters, we measure deletion rates directly at the user and repository level, and
find annualized deletion rates of 0.4% for user accounts and 5.8% for repositories. Deletion
therefore affects only a small fraction of the activity we observe.

6     Productivity Effects Across the Production Hierarchy
Having documented large task-level productivity effects that grow across successive gen-
erations of AI tools, we next ask how these task-level gains translate into final output.
We do so by examining final-output measures directly—the number of repositories and
releases—and we also use our model as a guide to understand the underlying mechanics:
if task-level gains fail to translate into final output as the weak-link hypothesis predicts,
we should see the effect decrease as we move to higher levels of task aggregation. To test
this, we look at the effect of AI tool adoption on outcomes at each layer of the production
hierarchy: lines of code, files, commits, pull requests, repositories, and releases.

6.1   How Does Task-Level Productivity Affect Final Output?
In Table 6, we find a pattern consistent with this prediction. The columns correspond
to progressively higher layers of the production hierarchy, from lines of code through
releases. Across all three generations of tools, the effects are largest for lines of code,
smaller for files, commits, and pull requests, and smaller still for repositories and releases.
   Starting with autocomplete, the effects decline monotonically across layers: from
234.3% for lines of code, to 30.2% for commits, and to only 9.0% for releases. This is
precisely the pattern predicted by Corollary 1: because autocomplete operates only at the
code-writing layer (Section 3), its gains must pass through every higher layer, and each
layer, bottlenecked by human input, absorbs part of the gain. Moving to sync agents, the
same monotonic pattern holds at larger magnitudes: the effect on lines of code is 957.5%,

falling to 47.3% for repositories and 19.8% for releases. The decay across layers looks
similar when we examine individual sync tools rather than the pooled effect.
    For async agents, we do not report a release effect: releases are measured at the
repository level and cannot be split into agent- and human-authored components. Across
the remaining outcomes, the effects again decline across layers with one exception: the
effect on pull requests is 68.5%, slightly larger than the 60.7% effect on commits. This
pattern is consistent with async agents being particularly effective at the PR layer: they
create pull requests autonomously, directly raising productivity at that layer rather than
merely passing upstream gains through it.
   Indeed, the model does not necessarily imply a strictly decreasing pattern for sync and
async agents, since they enter at multiple layers: if they are more effective at higher layers,
the effect size can in principle increase rather than decrease across layers. Either way, with
multiple entry points, the model predicts dampened attenuation relative to a shock that
enters only at the code-writing layer, as autocomplete does. We revisit this in Section 6.3
by mapping the empirical results directly to the model.

    Figure 7 presents the corresponding event studies for the sync effect across all outcomes;
the analogous per-tool event studies across all outcomes are reported in Figures OA-5–
OA-10 in the Appendix. Several features are worth noting. First, pre-trends are flat for
all outcomes, supporting the parallel trends assumption. Second, the effects on lines of
code, files, and commits are increasing from roughly week 5 onward, consistent with the
patterns in the main commit-level event studies and the opposite of the short-lived activity
bias pattern of the placebo event studies. Third, the result for releases is the least visually


                                                                                 34
 sharp: rather than a clear discontinuity at adoption, the effect builds gradually, which is
 consistent with the lag between writing code and shipping a release.
     How do these magnitudes compare with other evidence? Our estimates are larger
 than those from studies of firm-level adoption: against our pooled sync effects of 153% on
 commits and 958% on lines of code, Chen and Stratton (2026) and Sarkar (2026) find that
 agent adoption raises commits or merges by 20% and 39%, and lines of code by 30% and
 49%, respectively.25 Smaller firm-level effects likely reflect organizational inertia: realizing
 the gains from AI tools requires complementary investments that take time (Bresnahan
 et al., 2002; Brynjolfsson et al., 2021), whereas the open-source developers we study work
 individually or in small teams and face less of this friction. By contrast, our estimates
 are closer to the rapidly growing gains that the frontier AI labs—organizations that have
 plausibly already made these complementary investments—report for their own engineers.
 For example, OpenAI reports that lines of code changed per developer rose roughly 600%
 between 2024 and mid-2026, and Anthropic reports that its engineers merged about 700%
 more code per day in the second quarter of 2026 than in 2024. Meta’s reported numbers
 even mirror our attenuation pattern: internal code changes were up 220% year-over-year,
 while changes reaching users through new or upgraded features were up only 36%.26
     To understand the robustness of the attenuation results, we use the placebo analysis
 and the alternative specifications described in Section 5.5. The placebo analysis (Ta-
 ble OA-10) implements the same empirical specification for non-AI coding tools and finds
 no attenuation. Under alternative specifications (level regressions, estimation on a com-
 mon sample of developers active in every outcome, and alternative winsorization), the
 gradient’s magnitude shifts somewhat but the attenuation is always present (Appendix
 Tables OA-7–OA-9). We further rule out code verbosity as an explanation for the atten-
 uation: agents can write more verbose code, inflating counts of lines and files without a
 commensurate rise in substantive output. We examine verbosity empirically by looking at
 the share of comment lines in releases—the clearest symptom of AI verbosity—and find
 no upward trend between 2021 and 2026 (Table 7, Panel B). Moreover, verbosity cannot
 inflate counts of repositories or releases, yet we observe attenuation for these as well.

 6.2   Mechanisms Underlying Attenuation
 The previous section documented substantial attenuation of AI’s productivity effects along
 the production hierarchy. We now examine two mechanisms that can generate this pattern:
25Timing may explain part of the gap: their samples end in March 2026 and January 2026, respectively, while
  ours runs later, and our evidence suggests that the effects are growing rapidly.
26OpenAI, “Research acceleration: The view inside OpenAI,” September 6, 2026; Anthropic, “When AI builds
  itself,” May 2026; Katie Paul, “Mark Zuckerberg had a bold plan to replace Meta staff with AI. Here’s how it
  imploded,” Reuters, August 26, 2026.


                                                      35
experimentation, whereby AI induces additional activity at the lower layers that never
becomes shipped output, and scope, whereby the gains do reach the higher layers but
accrue through the increased scope of each unit of output rather than in their number.
    Which mechanism drives the attenuation matters for how we interpret it in light of our
model. If the attenuation arises through scope—more inputs are generated at each layer
and are packed into units of greater scope at the later stages, for example releases em-
bodying more features—then the productivity gains are partially realized on the intensive
margin, and the raw counts we use as outcomes do not fully reflect actual output. In this
case, part of the measured attenuation is an artifact of measurement rather than evidence
of bottlenecks.
    The experimentation channel, by contrast, maps directly into the model and its weak-
link hypothesis. If the attenuation comes from experimentation, it reflects AI expanding
what developers generate at the earlier stages, while human review, quality control, and
release capacity limit what reaches the final product. This can happen at several stages
of production: developers may generate multiple versions of a piece of code in several
iterations but later delete or abandon some of them; open branches to explore features that
never make it into the final product; or create repositories to explore ideas they never ship.
   Abandonment does not necessarily mean that the discarded work was of low quality:
under complementarities, developers simply lack the bandwidth to process every candi-
date at the later stages, where human input is essential and AI is less effective, so they may
simply be choosing among equally good alternatives based on taste, or keeping the best
draw from a distribution. These abandoned coding activities are still an input to the pro-
duction function, since the final output relies on generating and selecting among them, and
our event-study measures include them: commits, branches, and repositories are counted
whether or not the work ships. The experimentation implication of complementarities is
consistent with the broader narrative of how software development is changing, especially
the growing use of agents for rapid prototyping and iteration (Google, 2025).
    We now turn from describing the two channels to measuring them. To gauge the role
of scope, we examine whether the units at the higher layers have grown larger: we repeat
the event study of Section 5.2 using lines per commit and files per commit as outcomes,
and we also directly examine end-to-end attenuation by counting the number of lines of
code contained in releases over time.
    We examine evidence of experimentation at three levels of the hierarchy: (i) iteration at
the code level, such as rewriting existing code, which indicates experimentation at the level
of small tasks; (ii) branches that are created and deleted, which reflect experimentation
at the feature level; and (iii) projects that are abandoned, which reflect experimentation

at the level of ideas—starting a project to try something out and later abandoning it. For
code-level iteration, we implement our event study with lines of code added and lines
deleted separately as outcomes: an increase in deletions would indicate that the rise in
gross lines translates into a smaller rise in net lines. For experimentation at the branch
level, we examine the number of branches created and deleted and compare them with the
other outcomes. For abandoned projects, we report the share of repositories that receive no
activity after their first month, by creation cohort. Table 7 presents the results, separately
for autocomplete and for pooled sync agents.
    Starting with scope, the first two columns of Panel A in Table 7 report the effects on
lines and files per commit: lines per commit rise by 33% and files per commit by 6% for
autocomplete, and by 67% and 22% for sync agents. The end-to-end measure—the amount
of code contained in released software, shown in Panel B—increases only by a factor of


                                                         37
 about 2.6, from 5.21 to 13.42 thousand median lines per release, and the median number
 of files per release rises even less, by a factor of about 1.6 (from 48 to 76). Because these
 statistics are computed on the repositories of the adopters themselves, this modest growth
 is not an artifact of incomplete adoption: the comparison is not diluted by repositories of
 developers who never adopted AI tools. Thus, while the units at the higher layers have
 become larger, the magnitude of this increase is nowhere near what would be required to
 account for the attenuation pattern we document.
     Turning to experimentation, the third and fourth columns of Panel A show that dele-
 tions increase by a factor of 12.2, whereas additions increase only by a factor of 7.0,
 suggesting a substantial rise in the reworking of code. The last two columns of Panel A
 report a 79% increase in the number of branches created, providing direct evidence of
 experimentation at the feature level, since branches are most likely to be created when a
 developer tries something new. Finally, the first row of Panel B shows that the share of
 repositories that are abandoned rises by 9 percentage points between the 2021 and 2026
 cohorts. The rise accelerates with the arrival of agentic tools: the share increases by about
 1.2 percentage points per year over 2021–2024, and by 3.9 percentage points in 2025 alone.27
     Taken together, these results suggest that increased scope explains only a small fraction
 of the attenuation; most of it instead reflects experimentation at multiple layers of the
 production hierarchy. AI enables developers to try far more, while human bottlenecks at
 the later stages filter out most of these attempts.

 6.3    Quantifying Complementarities in Software Production
 We have established that productivity gains attenuate across layers, and we have provided
 suggestive evidence that this attenuation reflects complementarities and bottlenecks along
 the production chain. We now turn to quantifying these complementarities using the
 production model of Section 3. We showed there that the elasticity of substitution between
 upstream and effective input is the key parameter governing the shape of attenuation across
 layers. We can therefore invert this relationship, using our estimated attenuation pattern
 to back out the structural parameters that govern how task-level productivity translates
 into final output. We note that our production function is highly stylized, and we do
 not interpret this exercise as identification of the actual production function. However,
 mapping the data into these parameters provides suggestive evidence on the potential
 complementarities.
       To do this, we rely on Proposition 1, which showed that pass-through at each layer is
27To avoid censoring at the right end of the sample, the 2026 cohort includes only repositories created in the
  first three months of the year, leaving a buffer of at least three months after each repository’s first month
  closes to detect abandonment.


                                                      38
governed by two parameters: 𝜃, the output elasticity of each layer with respect to upstream
input, and 𝜎, the elasticity of substitution between upstream output and effective input.
Our goal is to find values of (𝜃, 𝜎) that, applied to the model, reproduce the empirical
attenuation pattern.
    We perform this calibration using the autocomplete estimates alone. Autocomplete
enters the hierarchy only at the code-writing layer, so the model’s prediction is simple:
we take the empirical lines-of-code effect as the layer-1 shock and apply the pass-through
formula recursively to obtain predicted gains at each higher layer. Sync and async agents,
in contrast, intervene at multiple layers (Section 3), and we cannot separately identify their
layer-specific productivity inputs from the attenuation pattern alone. For tractability we
assume 𝜃 and 𝜎 are constant across layers, leaving two free parameters. We then choose
(𝜃, 𝜎) to minimize the sum of squared log differences between predicted and empirical
gains at all six layers. Appendix D.8 provides the formal details of the calibration exercise.
    How are these two parameters identified from the data? They affect the attenuation
pattern in qualitatively different ways, and both signatures are visible empirically. The
parameter 𝜃 shifts the overall rate of decay: a higher 𝜃 means upstream input has a
larger output elasticity, so the same upstream gain propagates more strongly through each
layer. The parameter 𝜎 shapes the decay through the share of the log gain that survives
from one layer to the next. When 𝜎 < 1 (complements), this share rises across layers:
the layers just above the shock absorb a disproportionate share of the gain, and what
survives propagates more easily thereafter. When 𝜎 = 1, it is constant at 𝜃. When 𝜎 > 1
(substitutes), it falls across layers (Proposition 2). The overall steepness of the empirical
decay therefore identifies 𝜃, and the way this surviving share moves across layers identifies
𝜎.
    Figure 8 shows the empirical autocomplete attenuation pattern and the model pre-
diction at the best-fit parameters, 𝜃 ≈ 0.76 and 𝜎 ≈ 0.23. The estimated elasticity of
substitution is well below one: upstream output and effective input are strong comple-
ments, combined in roughly fixed proportions. This is why the attenuation we observe is
steep and front-loaded. The estimate of 𝜃 implies that at each layer output has an elasticity
of roughly 0.76 with respect to upstream input and roughly 0.24 with respect to the layer’s
own effective input.
   We next construct a counterfactual prediction for sync and async agents under the
assumption that these tools affect only lines of code (layer 1). For each tool, we take its
empirical lines-of-code effect as the layer-1 shock and apply the recursive pass-through
formula with the (𝜃, 𝜎) estimated from autocomplete. Figure 9 compares these counterfac-
tual predictions with the empirical effects. For both sync and async agents, the empirical

                          Figure 8: Calibrated Model Fit for Autocomplete

                         Productivity gain (%)

Notes: This figure shows the calibrated model’s fit to the autocomplete attenuation pattern (𝜃 = 0.76, 𝜎 = 0.23).
Blue circles show the empirical effects at each layer; black squares show the model prediction.


effects lie above the predicted gains at every intermediate layer. These deviations are con-
sistent with sync and async agents intervening directly at higher layers of the production
hierarchy, not merely passing through their layer-1 effect.
   To further understand the role of complementarities, we repeat the calibration for the
two alternative models of attenuation developed in Section 3.5 and Appendix B, using the
same procedure: a Cobb–Douglas production function, which has no complementarities,
and a CES specification with decreasing returns to scale. Both alternatives fail, for different
reasons: the Cobb–Douglas loss is more than 100 times the baseline’s, while the decreasing-
returns specification matches the pattern only with economically implausible parameter
values (Appendix Table OA-1). We therefore conclude that complementarities are the
primary channel behind the attenuation pattern.
    We also ask whether developers reallocate their inputs across layers in response to the
productivity shock, one of the extensions of the model. The data provide no support for
full reoptimization: allowing reallocation does not improve the fit. This suggests that the
reallocation margin does not operate in the short run and takes time, consistent with J-curve
dynamics, in which the gains from a new technology materialize only after complementary
adjustments (Brynjolfsson et al., 2021). Such a delay is natural in our setting: we study the
period immediately after these tools became available, before developers could plausibly
have completed such adjustments.
   The results in this section show that productivity effects attenuate from lines of code
toward final output, in a pattern consistent with complementarities and driven by exper-
imentation. This provides direct empirical evidence for the weak-link hypothesis widely

discussed in the macroeconomic productivity literature (Jones, 2011; Aghion et al., 2019;
Jones, 2026). At the same time, the bottleneck is moving up the production chain as AI
capabilities advance—autocomplete intervenes only at the code-writing layer, while sync
and async agents intervene at progressively higher layers—so the gap between task-level
effects and effects on shipped software may narrow over time as tools automate higher
layers of the hierarchy.

7                         Aggregate Evidence from GitHub and Software Marketplaces
Our developer-level results document substantial productivity gains from AI coding tools,
with effects that attenuate sharply across the production hierarchy. A natural question is
whether these micro-level effects translate into aggregate changes in software production.
This question echoes a long-standing debate in economics. In 1987, Robert Solow famously
observed that “you can see the computer age everywhere but in the productivity statistics”
(Solow, 1987). Decades later, the same concern has resurfaced with AI. While individual
studies document large task-level productivity gains (e.g., Peng et al., 2023; Noy and Zhang,
2023; Brynjolfsson et al., 2025; Cui et al., 2026), whether these gains will reach aggregate
output remains an open question (Acemoglu, 2025; Jones, 2026; Karger et al., 2026).
   We examine this question in two parts. First, we study aggregate coding activity on
GitHub. This analysis provides a direct aggregate counterpart to our developer-level event
studies. Second, we turn to the output of software on major distribution marketplaces,
examining whether the productivity gains we document translate into more apps being
created and, ultimately, used by end users.

7.1                   Aggregate Coding Activity on GitHub
We begin by establishing that aggregate adoption of AI coding tools on GitHub is large
enough to make our developer-level effects potentially detectable in platform-wide totals.
Figure 10(a) plots the share of commits on public repositories that are signed or authored
by just one agentic coding tool: Claude Code. The share has risen rapidly since the tool’s
release in early 2025 and by mid-2026 exceeds 7% of all public commits. Since developers
frequently commit AI-generated work under their own name and use many tools beyond
Claude Code, this is almost certainly an undercount of total AI-assisted coding activity.
                   Figures 10(b) and 10(c) turn to aggregate platform activity directly, plotting monthly

                                                                                        42
 counts of merged pull requests and newly created (non-fork) public repositories from 2019
 through mid-2026. Both series grow roughly linearly from 2019 through 2024, in line
 with steady organic platform growth. Around early 2025, this trend breaks: both series
 accelerate sharply relative to the pre-2025 trend. The acceleration in merged pull requests
 is also visibly larger than the acceleration in new repositories created, consistent with the
 attenuation pattern documented in Section 6.28

 7.2     New Apps and Usage in Software Marketplaces
 In contrast to aggregate productivity in the broader economy, software output is reason-
 ably observable: software marketplaces record when new apps are published and how
 widely they are used. We use this transparency to ask whether the developer-level pro-
 ductivity gains documented above translate into measurable changes in software output
 and software consumption.

 7.2.1    Data and Measurement

 We assemble monthly panels for four of the largest software marketplaces: the Apple App
 Store (iOS), the Google Play Store (Android), the Chrome Web Store (browser extensions),
 and SourceForge (a legacy platform for desktop software). Together these four span
 different developer populations, from mobile consumer apps to browser extensions and
 desktop software.
    For each marketplace we observe each app’s first-seen date and cumulative end-user
 usage thereafter. The usage measure is the rating count for iOS (where we do not directly
 observe installs) and the cumulative install count for the other marketplaces. Using these
 data, we construct monthly cohorts, each consisting of the non-incumbent apps that first
 appeared in that month, and we track the usage they attract within their first three months.
 This window is short enough to include cohorts near the end of our sample and long
 enough that the usage signal is meaningful. Our results are robust to other windows.

 7.2.2    Releases of New Applications

 The top row of Figure 11 plots monthly counts of new releases for each marketplace. To
 help the reader assess whether agentic coding accelerated new releases, we highlight the
28GitHub itself has pointed to this surge: in a postmortem of a major outage on August 17, 2026, GitHub’s
  CTO reported that monthly commits had more than doubled since April 2026, from 1.4 to 2.9 billion, and
  cited this growth as the source of the capacity strain behind the outage. See GitHub Blog—The August 17
  outage and the work ahead.

 period from February 2025 onward as the agentic-coding era on each plot.29

     All three platforms show either an acceleration in entry or a break from a prior decline,
 aligned broadly in time with the rise of agentic coding tools. The Apple App Store shows
 the clearest acceleration. New iOS apps run between roughly 33,000 and 45,000 per
 month from 2023 through early 2025, then rise sharply over the course of 2025, reaching
 approximately 108,000 per month by April 2026. The acceleration further gathers pace
 in early 2026, consistent with the Claude Code effect rising over time as the underlying
 model improves (Figure 5(b)).
     The Google Play Store shows a different pattern. Monthly new releases had declined
 steadily from a 2020 peak of about 145,000 to about 42,000 in January 2025, and the
 trajectory then breaks: releases stabilize and recover through the end of our sample,
 reaching about 99,000 per month by mid-2026. The Chrome Web Store shows a slower
 but visible acceleration that predates the agentic coding era, with new extensions rising
 roughly 7-fold since 2023, though growth picks up further with the arrival of coding agents
 in 2025.30

 7.2.3   Consumption of New Apps

 We now investigate whether the increase in new releases translates into additional con-
 sumption, which is informative about, though not a direct measure of, consumer surplus.
 We first examine total cohort-level usage. The middle row of Figure 11 plots, for each
 entry month, the sum of usage over the next three months across all apps in that cohort.
 If the additional apps had generated meaningful consumption, the aggregate would rise
 with the size of the cohort. Instead, aggregate usage fails to keep pace in all three mar-
 ketplaces: iOS cohort ratings are roughly stable between 2024 and 2025, Android cohort
 downloads rise only modestly (far less than the number of new releases), and Chrome
 cohort downloads fall rapidly, though they had already been falling in the pre-period.
     The fact that aggregate usage does not rise with entry rules out large increases in
 consumer surplus from the introduction of new blockbuster apps. It also rules out a “long-
 tail” surplus channel, in which a large number of newly released niche apps each attract
 a small audience but, integrated across the cohort, generate substantial total consumer
 surplus (Brynjolfsson et al., 2003). Under the long-tail story, each app’s user base would be
 small but the aggregate would rise with the number of new releases. While this channel
29We choose this date because GitHub launched Copilot Agent Mode, among the first widely used agentic
  coding products, in late February 2025; other agentic tools followed soon after. See the announcement from
  Visual Studio Code.
30We report SourceForge in Appendix Figure OA-17 because its download counts are too small to support a
  meaningful analysis of usage; we therefore show only the number of new projects, which increases slightly.


                                                     45
 has been documented for AI-written books on Amazon (Reimers and Waldfogel, 2026), it
 is not visible in the software marketplaces we study.
     Still, flat aggregate usage does not by itself imply that consumer surplus is unaffected,
 as the introduction of additional apps could have improved matching between consumers
 and apps. From a consumer’s perspective, apps are partial substitutes, since time spent on
 any one app is bounded by total leisure time, so an additional new app need not raise total
 usage. The new apps could still raise consumer surplus by letting each consumer choose
 the variant best suited to her preferences, with the gains showing up as better matches
 rather than more usage.
     To assess the matching channel, we examine the share of each monthly cohort that
 fails to reach a moderate audience within three months. We classify an app as failing to
 reach an audience if it accumulates fewer than 10 ratings (iOS), fewer than 10 downloads
 (Chrome), or at most 100 downloads (Android) in its first three months.31 The bottom row
 of Figure 11 plots the time path of this share (in green) and its complement (in dark red)
 for each marketplace, level-shifted to zero in January 2025. In all three marketplaces the
 share of releases that fail to reach an audience trends upward in the agentic era: between
 January 2025 and April 2026, it rises from about 78% to 87% on iOS, from 19% to 33%
 on Chrome, and from about 22% to 26% on Android. If the additional apps were better-
 matched substitutes, users switching to them would generate at least the modest demand
 needed to clear the threshold; instead, the marginal apps are increasingly invisible to end
 users. This argues against the matching channel as well.

 7.2.4   Discussion

 The marketplace evidence has two parts. New app releases have accelerated, while cohort-
 level usage has not. The first finding is consistent with the hierarchy model of Section 3:
 entry has increased across marketplaces, but by less than the task-level productivity esti-
 mates would imply. These increases nevertheless indicate that the rise in coding activity
 we document on GitHub has reached the marketplaces.32
     The second pattern, that usage fails to keep pace with entry, admits multiple explana-
 tions. One reading is supply-side: AI relaxes the constraints on producing and publishing
 software, so more projects reach publication. But publication need not be the binding layer
31We use platform-specific thresholds because usage intensity differs substantially across marketplaces; a
  uniform cutoff would push the small-audience share close to zero or one in some marketplaces, leaving little
  variation to study.
32The effects on the number of new apps in the marketplaces are larger than the developer-level release
  event-study estimates. This is partly because GitHub is observed earlier than the software marketplaces:
  the marketplace data end in August 2026, whereas the GitHub estimation panel ends in mid-April 2026
  (Appendix D.3), and the 30-week post-adoption windows fall even earlier for early adopters.


                                                      46
 for market-valued output: developers must still test, polish, and iterate toward market fit,
 and these higher-level tasks may remain constrained even when AI lowers the cost of
 writing code. The marketplace evidence would then be the attenuation pattern writ large:
 gains at upstream layers raise the flow of releases, but usage-weighted output rises much
 less.
     An alternative explanation, which we cannot rule out, is demand-side congestion. Even
 if the quality of new apps were unchanged, user attention and discovery channels (search
 rankings, recommendations, word of mouth) are scarce and need not expand with the
 number of entrants. As more apps compete for a fixed pool of attention, average per-app
 usage falls. This is not a production bottleneck, but it looks the same in the data: more
 publications without proportionally more usage.33

 8    Conclusion
 The capabilities of generative AI tools for software development have advanced rapidly,
 moving from autocomplete to fully autonomous coding agents in just a few years. Do
 these capability gains translate into higher real-world task-level productivity? And even if
 they do, do task-level gains translate into more final output? These questions are central
 to understanding AI’s economic impact. In developer-level matched event studies, we
 find that successive generations of AI coding tools produce increasingly large task-level
 productivity effects: autocomplete raises weekly commits by roughly 30%, sync agents
 raise the cumulative effect to roughly 180%, and async agents to roughly 240%. Yet
 these gains attenuate sharply across the production hierarchy: sync agents lead to a 958%
 increase in lines of code and an 86% increase in pull requests, but releases rise by only
 20%. Aggregate coding activity on GitHub mirrors this pattern.
    To interpret this attenuation, we introduce a hierarchical model of software production
 in which output is produced through the sequential aggregation of tasks. The model
 highlights the elasticity of substitution between upstream output and downstream hu-
 man effort as the key parameter governing how task-level gains propagate to final output.
 Calibrating the model to our estimates yields an elasticity of 0.23, pointing to strong com-
 plementarity between AI and human inputs along the production chain. Direct evidence
 on mechanisms supports this reading: the attenuation reflects experimentation that is fil-
33Several caveats about our analysis are worth noting. First, our sample runs only through mid-2026, leaving
  under a year and a half of post-launch data for agentic tools; the usage response may simply be slow, since
  discovery and adoption take time and supply-side learning from user feedback requires multiple product
  iterations. Second, we cannot fully separate productivity-driven entry from demand-driven entry; some
  new apps may have been written specifically to serve AI-related demand. Third, our demand measures are
  imperfect proxies for consumer surplus: ratings and downloads need not move one-for-one with the value
  an app generates for its users, particularly for free apps.


                                                     47
tered out at human-bottlenecked stages (more reworking of code, more branches created,
more projects abandoned), rather than larger units of output.
    The same logic motivates our analysis of software marketplaces: if AI increases the
supply of software, does that software reach users, and is it actually used? Across all four
marketplaces, new app creation has accelerated since early 2025, but total usage has not kept
pace, and the share of new apps that fail to reach even a modest audience has increased.
This pattern admits two interpretations: either higher production layers (testing, polishing,
iterating toward market fit) still bottleneck market-valued output, mirroring in aggregate
the attenuation we document, or a consumer-side bottleneck (discovery and adoption)
prevents increased supply from translating into usage.
    Our evidence comes from open-source development, but the pattern extends to firms.
Chen and Stratton (2026), using proprietary data on enterprise software development, find
the same attenuation, with code review as the binding constraint. If anything, these bot-
tlenecks should bind more tightly inside firms, where shipping code involves review and
approval processes that open-source developers largely avoid, and easing these bottlenecks
will require complementary organizational investments (Brynjolfsson et al., 2021).
    Taken together, our results provide empirical evidence for a more general principle.
When stages of production are complementary, automating one stage has bounded effects
on final output: a vertical analog of the “weak links” logic in the macroeconomic growth
literature. This offers one resolution of the tension between large task-level productivity
estimates and muted aggregate projections: both can hold at once when the stages of pro-
duction are strong complements. In software, the binding constraint appears to be shifting
from writing code to reviewing, integrating, and distributing it. Whether future gener-
ations of AI tools can ease these downstream constraints—by producing higher-quality
code that requires less human review, automating review and integration, or improving
discovery and adoption—will determine whether the large task-level productivity gains
we document ultimately translate into commensurate increases in shipped and used soft-
ware.

A.2   Derivation of the Pass-Through Formula

Consider a single layer of production combining upstream input 𝑥 with effective effort 𝑒
via a CES technology:

                                                                                𝜎−1
                            𝑦 = [𝛼 𝑥 𝜌 + (1 − 𝛼) 𝑒 𝜌 ]1/𝜌 ,            𝜌=           ,              (12)
                                                                                 𝜎

where 𝜎 > 0 is the elasticity of substitution and 𝛼 ∈ (0, 1) is the share parameter. In the
context of the main model, 𝑥 = 𝑦 𝑠−1 and 𝑒 = 𝑒 𝑠 . The output elasticity of upstream input is

                                         𝜕 ln 𝑦          𝛼 𝑥𝜌
                                    𝜃≡          =                     .                            (13)
                                         𝜕 ln 𝑥   𝛼 𝑥 𝜌 + (1 − 𝛼) 𝑒 𝜌

    A productivity shock multiplies upstream input by 𝐴 > 1, so 𝑥˜ = 𝐴𝑥, while effective
effort 𝑒 is unchanged. The new output is

                                    𝑦˜ = [𝛼 𝐴𝜌 𝑥 𝜌 + (1 − 𝛼) 𝑒 𝜌 ]1/𝜌 .

Dividing by 𝑦 and using 𝛼 𝑥 𝜌 = 𝜃 [𝛼 𝑥 𝜌 + (1 − 𝛼) 𝑒 𝜌 ]:

                                                   1/𝜌
                       𝑦˜   𝛼 𝑥 𝜌 𝐴𝜌 + (1 − 𝛼) 𝑒 𝜌
                                                    
                          =                            = [𝜃 𝐴𝜌 + (1 − 𝜃)]1/𝜌 ,                     (14)
                       𝑦     𝛼 𝑥 𝜌 + (1 − 𝛼) 𝑒 𝜌


                                                   OA - 3
which is the formula in Equation (8). The pass-through depends only on the output
elasticity 𝜃 and the elasticity of substitution 𝜎; the share parameter 𝛼 enters only through
𝜃.

A.3    Proof of Proposition 1

Let 𝑓 (𝐴, 𝜃, 𝜎) ≡ [𝜃 𝐴𝜌 + (1 − 𝜃)]1/𝜌 with 𝜌 = (𝜎 − 1)/𝜎 denote the single-layer pass-through.

Proof of Proposition 1. (a) 𝜕 𝑓 /𝜕𝜎 > 0. The pass-through 𝑓 (𝐴, 𝜃, 𝜎) = [𝜃𝐴𝜌 + (1 − 𝜃)]1/𝜌 is a
power mean of 𝐴 and 1 with weights 𝜃 and 1 − 𝜃 and order 𝜌 = (𝜎 − 1)/𝜎. Power means
are strictly increasing in their order, and 𝜌 is strictly increasing in 𝜎, so 𝜕 𝑓 /𝜕𝜎 > 0 for all
𝐴 > 1 and 𝜃 ∈ (0, 1).
    (b) 𝜕 𝑓 /𝜕𝜃 > 0.
                                 𝜕𝑓  1                 1
                                    = [𝜃 𝐴𝜌 + (1 − 𝜃)] 𝜌 (𝐴𝜌 − 1) .
                                                         −1
                                 𝜕𝜃 𝜌
When 𝜌 > 0 (𝜎 > 1), 𝐴𝜌 > 1 and 1/𝜌 > 0, so 𝜕 𝑓 /𝜕𝜃 > 0. When 𝜌 < 0 (𝜎 < 1), 𝐴𝜌 < 1 and
1/𝜌 < 0, so the product is again positive. In the Cobb–Douglas limit (𝜌 → 0), 𝑓 = 𝐴𝜃 and
𝜕 𝑓 /𝜕𝜃 = 𝐴𝜃 ln 𝐴 > 0.                                                                 □

A.4    Proof of Corollary 1

Proof of Corollary 1. Let 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) = [𝜃 𝑗 𝑥 𝜌 𝑗 + (1 − 𝜃 𝑗 )]1/𝜌 𝑗 denote the single-layer pass-through at
layer 𝑗, with 𝜃 𝑗 ∈ (0, 1) and 𝜌 𝑗 = (𝜎 𝑗 − 1)/𝜎 𝑗 ; in the Cobb–Douglas case (𝜌 𝑗 → 0), 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) = 𝑥 𝜃𝑗 .
    (i) For any 𝑥 > 1, 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) is a weighted power mean of 𝑥 and 1 with strictly positive
weights 𝜃 𝑗 and 1 − 𝜃 𝑗 , so it lies strictly between its arguments: 1 < 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) < 𝑥. (Strictness
of attenuation is where the requirement that effective input have positive weight at every
layer, 𝜃 𝑗 < 1, is used; 𝜃 𝑗 > 0 ensures the gain remains strictly positive. The Cobb–Douglas
case satisfies the same inequalities since 1 < 𝑥 𝜃𝑗 < 𝑥.) Holding inputs at all other layers
fixed, a shock 𝐴 > 1 at layer 𝑘 raises output at layer 𝑠 > 𝑘 by the composition of the
intervening pass-throughs,

                                      𝑦˜ 𝑠
                                           = 𝑓𝑠 ◦ 𝑓𝑠−1 ◦ · · · ◦ 𝑓 𝑘+1 (𝐴),
                                      𝑦𝑠

which follows by applying Equation (14) recursively, treating the gain inherited from layer
𝑗 − 1 as the upstream shock to layer 𝑗. Since each application of 𝑓 𝑗 maps its argument 𝑥 > 1
into (1, 𝑥), the cumulative gain is strictly decreasing in 𝑠 and, in particular, strictly smaller
than the effect 𝐴 at layer 𝑘 for every 𝑠 > 𝑘.
   (ii) Each 𝑓 𝑗 is strictly increasing on (0, ∞): 𝑓 𝑗′(𝑥) = 𝜃 𝑗 𝑥 𝜌 𝑗 −1 [𝜃 𝑗 𝑥 𝜌 𝑗 + (1 − 𝜃 𝑗 )]1/𝜌 𝑗 −1 > 0.
The final-output gain from a technology entering at layer 𝑘 is 𝐸 𝑘 = 𝑓𝑆 ◦ · · · ◦ 𝑓 𝑘+1 (𝐴), and

                                                    OA - 4
from entering at layer 𝑘 ′ > 𝑘 it is 𝐸 𝑘′ = 𝑓𝑆 ◦ · · · ◦ 𝑓 𝑘′+1 (𝐴). By part (i), 𝑓 𝑘′ ◦ · · · ◦ 𝑓 𝑘+1 (𝐴) < 𝐴.
Applying the strictly increasing composite map 𝑓𝑆 ◦· · ·◦ 𝑓 𝑘′+1 to both sides of this inequality
yields 𝐸 𝑘 < 𝐸 𝑘′ .                                                                                          □

A.5      Proof of Proposition 2

Proof of Proposition 2(a). The proposition considers the symmetric case with common 𝜎
and 𝜃 at every layer. Let 𝐿 𝑠 = ln 𝐺 𝑠 , so 𝑅 𝑠 = 𝐿 𝑠+1 /𝐿 𝑠 . We show that 𝑅 𝑠 is monotone in 𝑠
with the sign claimed in the proposition.
    Step 1: Pointwise elasticity. The elasticity of the single-layer pass-through 𝑓 (𝑥) = [𝜃𝑥 𝜌 +
(1 − 𝜃)]1/𝜌 is

                                  𝜂(𝑥) =         =                 ,
                                         𝜕 ln 𝑥    𝜃 𝑥 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜃)
with 𝜕𝜂/𝜕𝑥 = 𝜃(1 − 𝜃)𝜌 𝑥 𝜌−1 /[𝜃𝑥 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜃)]2 . The sign of this derivative is the sign of 𝜌:
𝜂 is strictly decreasing in 𝑥 when 𝜎 < 1 and strictly increasing when 𝜎 > 1.
   Step 2: Average elasticity. Define 𝜂(𝑥)
                                      ¯    = ln 𝑓 (𝑥)/ln 𝑥 for 𝑥 > 1. Since 𝑓 (1) = 1, ln 𝑓 (𝑥) =

    • 𝜎 = 1: 𝜂¯ ≡ 𝜃 ⇒ 𝑅 𝑠 = 𝜃 for all 𝑠.
    • 𝜎 > 1: 𝜂¯ increasing, 𝐺 𝑠 decreasing ⇒ 𝑅 𝑠 strictly decreasing in 𝑠.

Proof of Proposition 2(b). Taking 𝐴 → ∞ in the pass-through formula (14):
    • Complements (𝜎 < 1, 𝜌 < 0): as 𝐴 → ∞, 𝐴𝜌 → 0, so

                                           𝑦˜               𝜎

                                           𝑦 𝐴→∞

    • Substitutes (𝜎 > 1, 𝜌 > 0): 𝐴𝜌 → ∞, so 𝑦/𝑦
                                             ˜ → ∞.

                                                   OA - 5
A.6   Connection to Jones (2026)

The bound in Equation (15) coincides with the automation bound in Jones (2026), with the
share parameter 𝜃 in the role of Jones’s spending share. Substituting 𝜎 = 1/2 (𝜌 = −1)
gives exponent 𝜎/(𝜎 − 1) = −1, so

                                                             1
                             (1 − 𝜃)𝜎/(𝜎−1) = (1 − 𝜃)−1 =       ,
                                                            1−𝜃

which matches Jones’s formula with 𝜃 in the role of his spending share. More generally,
the bound at 𝜃 = 0.5 varies sharply with 𝜎:

                 𝜎      𝜎/(𝜎 − 1)   Bound (1 − 𝜃)𝜎/(𝜎−1)     Percent increase

                 0.25     −1/3              1.26                     26%
                 0.50      −1               2.00                    100%
                 0.75      −3               8.00                    700%

    With strong complements (𝜎 = 0.25), even infinite upstream input raises output by at
most 26%. At 𝜎 = 0.5, the illustrative case in Jones (2026), the maximum gain is a doubling.
The bound increases sharply as 𝜎 approaches the Cobb–Douglas knife-edge (𝜎 = 1), where
it disappears entirely.




                                           OA - 6
B     Alternative Models of Attenuation
This appendix develops three alternative models to the model in the main text. Each retains
the hierarchical structure of Section 3.1 and changes either the production technology or
the developer’s choice of inputs. For each we derive the implied pass-through and the
resulting attenuation result.

B.1   Decreasing Returns without Complementarity

Production proceeds through 𝑆 layers as in the baseline model, with the technology
replaced by Cobb–Douglas:

                                         𝛾
                  𝑦1 = 𝑒 1 ,              𝑠
                                𝑦 𝑠 = 𝑦 𝑠−1 𝑒 𝑠𝛿 𝑠 ,       𝛾𝑠 , 𝛿 𝑠 > 0,       𝑠 = 2, . . . , 𝑆,          (16)

with final output 𝑌 = 𝑦𝑆 . Define the scale elasticity and the relative upstream share

                                                 𝛾𝑠

Under constant returns it is a pure share; under decreasing returns it is the product of the
share and the scale elasticity. This decomposition is the source of every difference below.

                                                       OA - 7
Remark 1 (Attenuation without a human bottleneck). The attenuation result of Corol-
lary 1 of the main model survives, but with its hypothesis weakened. In the baseline
model, a layer attenuates because additional upstream output must be combined with the
downstream effective input; the weight of that input, together with its complementarity
with upstream output, determines the strength of the attenuation. Under decreasing re-
turns, a layer attenuates even when the effective input carries little weight and there is no
complementarity—indeed even with no effective input at all (𝜃¯ 𝑗 = 1)—with substantial
attenuation driven by decreasing returns alone.
                                                                                 ¯ 𝑠−𝑘 ln 𝐴,
Remark 2 (Shape of the decline). With parameters common across layers, ln 𝐺 𝑠 = (𝜃𝜇)
so the survival rate defined in Proposition 2 is

                                            ln 𝐺 𝑠+1
                                     𝑅𝑠 =            = 𝜃¯ 𝜇,                               (20)
                                             ln 𝐺 𝑠

constant across layers and invariant to the size of the shock. Decreasing returns therefore
change how much of the gain survives, but not the pattern in which it is lost: the propor-
tional decline is the same at every layer, regardless of the size of the gain that reaches it or
its position in the hierarchy.
Remark 3 (Transmission of infinite productivity effects). Proposition 2(b) shows that under
complements infinite productivity effects are not transmitted to final output: even an
unbounded improvement in AI delivers a finite gain, because human input at each layer
remains essential. Under decreasing returns, by contrast, infinite productivity effects are
transmitted: pass-through is 𝐴Γ𝑘,𝑆 with Γ 𝑘,𝑆 > 0, so final output grows without bound as
𝐴 → ∞.
                                                                                            ¯
Remark 4 (Identification). With decreasing returns, pass-through across a layer is 𝐴𝜃𝑠 𝜇𝑠 ,
so attenuation is driven by the product of the two parameters: the observed attenuation
identifies this product rather than the share and the scale elasticity separately.
Remark 5 (Allocation invariance). Under cost minimization with a Cobb–Douglas technol-
ogy, the allocation of expenditure across inputs is determined by the technology param-
eters alone and does not depend on input prices. As a result, the allocation of human
input does not change in response to AI productivity gains as long as input prices remain
the same. The assumption in the main model (Section 3.4) that the input allocation is
fixed—which we relax in Appendix B.3—is therefore without loss of generality under a
Cobb–Douglas production function.

Remark 6 (Comparison with the baseline model). Equation (23) makes the comparison with
the baseline model transparent: the pass-through under complementarity and decreasing
returns is the baseline pass-through raised to the power 𝜇𝑠 . The two forces compound, and
each attenuates the gain for a different reason: the share channel (𝜃¯ 𝑠 < 1) operates as in the
baseline model, since additional upstream output must be combined with the downstream
effective input, while the scale channel (𝜇𝑠 < 1) takes a further proportional cut of whatever
survives, because additional throughput runs into decreasing returns. Across layers, the

                                                      OA - 9
two play distinct roles. Taking logs in Equation (23) gives ln 𝐺 𝑠+1 = 𝜇 ln 𝐺CRS (𝐺 𝑠 ), so the
survival rate is 𝑅 𝑠 = 𝜇 𝑅 CRS (𝐺 𝑠 ), where 𝑅 CRS is the baseline survival function. The shape
classification of Proposition 2(a) is therefore preserved exactly—𝑅 𝑠 is increasing in 𝑠 when
𝜎 < 1, constant at 𝜇𝜃¯ when 𝜎 = 1, and decreasing when 𝜎 > 1—so complementarity
governs the shape of the attenuation profile, while decreasing returns shift its level from
𝜃¯ to 𝜇𝜃¯ without changing the sign of its slope.

B.3   Modeling Reallocation of Inputs across Layers

In this section, we relax the assumption of the baseline model that the allocation of devel-
oper effort across layers is fixed. Because reallocation is irrelevant under a Cobb–Douglas
technology (Remark 5), we work with the CES technology of Equation (2). The model is the
same as in the main text, with the only difference that the developer chooses her inputs at
every layer, subject to a fixed total expenditure, as AI changes productivity. Characterizing
this choice requires working with input prices, which we have abstracted from so far.
     Since augmentation and automation are perfect substitutes in producing effective input,

these equilibrium quantities via Shephard’s lemma.
Remark 8 (Comparison with the baseline model). Similar to the baseline model (Corol-
lary 1), attenuation is strict at every layer with 𝜃𝑠 < 1, so the attenuation results are robust
to the reallocation of inputs. However, different from the baseline model, reallocation
replaces every layer’s curvature 𝜌 𝑠 = (𝜎𝑠 − 1)/𝜎𝑠 with 𝜎𝑠 − 1. This replacement makes
attenuation weaker: for every layer and every 𝐴 > 1, 𝐺 𝑠 > 𝐺fixed   𝑠   (unless 𝜎 𝑗 = 1 at every
layer 𝑗 ≤ 𝑠), since the map 𝑥 ↦→ [𝜃𝑠 𝐺 𝑥 + (1 − 𝜃𝑠 )]1/𝑥 is a weighted power mean and hence
nondecreasing in 𝑥, and (𝜎𝑠 − 1) − 𝜌 𝑠 = (𝜎𝑠 − 1)2 /𝜎𝑠 ≥ 0. This is the Le Chatelier principle
(Samuelson, 1947; Milgrom and Roberts, 1996): allowing an input to adjust cannot lower
the response of output.
Remark 9 (Equivalent elasticity). Because the two recursions differ only in the curvature,
pass-through under frictionless reallocation has the fixed-allocation functional form with
𝜎𝑠 replaced by the equivalent elasticity

                                              1
                                   𝜎˜ 𝑠 =            (𝜎𝑠 < 2).                              (30)
                                            2 − 𝜎𝑠

Reallocation therefore makes adjacent layers behave as though more substitutable (𝜎𝑠 <
𝜎˜ 𝑠 < 1 for 𝜎𝑠 ∈ (0, 1)), but never converts complements into substitutes, since 𝜎˜ 𝑠 ≷ 1 if and
only if 𝜎𝑠 ≷ 1. Note also that 𝜎˜ 𝑠 ≥ 1/2 for every 𝜎𝑠 ≥ 0, with 𝜎˜ 𝑠 ↓ 1/2 as 𝜎𝑠 ↓ 0: even
a Leontief technology behaves, once the developer reoptimizes, as one with an elasticity
of one half. This again makes clear that our results on the role of complementarity in
attenuation are robust to reallocation.
Remark 10 (Direction of reallocation). If inputs are reallocated, from which layers and to
which layers do they move? Applying Shephard’s lemma to Equation (27), expenditure on
a layer’s own effective input relative to its upstream input responds to an AI improvement
with an elasticity governed by 𝜎𝑠 . Under complements (𝜎𝑠 < 1) better AI shifts expenditure,
and with it developer effort, downstream, toward the human-bottlenecked layer; under
substitutes it shifts them toward the layer where AI operates.



                                               OA - 12
 B.4    Simulated Attenuation Patterns

 The three models above, and their combinations, imply different attenuation patterns
 across the hierarchy. Using the results derived in the previous subsections, we now
 simulate the attenuation pattern of a productivity gain at the first layer, under the four
 technologies listed below:

                     Specification                                  Substitution      Scale

                     Cobb–Douglas, constant returns                     𝜎=1           𝜇=1
                     Cobb–Douglas, decreasing returns                   𝜎=1          𝜇 = 0.9
                     CES complements, constant returns                 𝜎 = 0.5        𝜇=1
                     CES complements, decreasing returns               𝜎 = 0.5       𝜇 = 0.9


     Common parameters are a baseline upstream share of 𝜃¯ = 0.7 at every layer, and a gain
 of 𝐴 = 2 (a doubling of effective coding input).35 Each specification is solved twice, giving
 eight combinations in total. Under fixed allocation, the baseline (𝐴 = 1) optimal allocation
 is held fixed when the gain arrives, as in Section 3.4. Under reallocation, the developer
 reoptimizes the entire allocation of inputs after the gain, holding total expenditure fixed,
 as in Appendix B.3.36
     Figure OA-1 plots, at every layer, the share of the layer-1 log gain surviving in measured
 layer output, ln( 𝑦˜ 𝑠 /𝑦 𝑠 )/ln 𝐴, with fixed allocation in Panel (a) and reallocation in Panel (b).
 Starting with Panel (a), the main pattern is that every specification attenuates, and steeply:
 only a small fraction of the layer-1 log gain reaches releases. Decreasing returns deepen
 the decline roughly uniformly, lowering survival at every layer under both technologies
 while leaving the shape of the profile unchanged, consistent with Propositions 3 and 4. The
 specifications are therefore distinguished less by how much attenuation they produce than
 by its shape across layers: the Cobb–Douglas per-layer survival rate is constant, whereas
 under CES complements it rises going up the hierarchy. Complementarity front-loads the
 attenuation, while decreasing returns spread it evenly.
     Panel (b) isolates the role of reallocation. Under Cobb–Douglas the profiles are identi-
 cal across panels, as Remark 5 implies: optimal effort shares do not depend on the size of
 the gain, so allowing reallocation changes nothing. Under CES, reallocation compresses
 the profile from both ends. The profiles in Panel (b) begin below one, because expenditure
35For the CES specifications the weights 𝛼 𝑠 are calibrated so that the baseline share equals 0.7 layer by layer;
  for Cobb–Douglas the exponents are 𝛾 = 0.7𝜇 and 𝛿 = 0.3𝜇.
36The simulations measure effective input at each layer in units whose unit cost is one at baseline and treat the
  gain as a fall in the unit cost of layer-1 input, so they hold for any split of that cost between developer time
  and AI output.

Notes: The figure plots the share of the layer-1 log gain surviving at each layer of the hierarchy, ln( 𝑦˜ 𝑠 /𝑦 𝑠 )/ln 𝐴,
following a doubling of effective coding input (𝐴 = 2) at layer 1. Each line is one of the four technologies described

reallocates from the coding layer to other layers, so the measured gain at the lines-of-code
layer understates the actual productivity gain. At the top of the hierarchy the ranking
reverses: the reallocation profiles end above their fixed-allocation counterparts. Realloca-
tion gives up measured gains low in the hierarchy in order to deliver more final output,
which is the Le Chatelier result of Appendix B.3.
    Overall, these simulation results show that our main results on attenuation, and its
relationship with complementarity, are robust to these alternative specifications. The
main effect of the alternative technologies is on the shape of the attenuation profile. In
the next section, we use our empirical attenuation estimates to assess how well these
alternative models fit the empirical pattern.

B.5                                         Taking the Alternative Models to the Data

Appendix B.4 shows that these models imply different attenuation patterns. We now
use our empirical estimates to assess how well each of them fits the data. To measure
the fit of the empirical pattern to each model, we repeat the estimation of Section 6.3,
minimizing the sum of squared log deviations between predicted and empirical gains,
for all eight specifications considered in the previous section—{Cobb–Douglas, CES} ×
{constant returns, decreasing returns} × {fixed allocation, reallocation}. As in the main
text, each specification is calibrated on autocomplete, because autocomplete delivers a

all six layers (×10−4 ). 𝐴ˆ Auto is the layer-1 gain: observed under fixed allocation (the lines gain, 1 + 234.3/100)
and estimated under reallocation. The four Cobb–Douglas rows are a single model: allocation invariance makes
the reallocation cells identical, and only 𝜃𝜇  ¯ is identified, so the decreasing-returns cells re-estimate the same
profile. The reallocation specifications hold total expenditure fixed, as in Appendix B.3. The CES specification
with constant returns and reallocation is computed in closed form using the recursive CES dual and Shephard’s
lemma.


productivity gain at the first layer only, which allows us to use its attenuation across the
layers above.
    Two specifications carry a caveat. First, under Cobb–Douglas the share and the scale
elasticity are not separately identified, only their product 𝜇𝜃¯ (Remark 4). Second, under
reallocation the layer-1 gain (𝐴) becomes a further free parameter, because the measured
lines response is an equilibrium outcome—the gain net of the expenditure the developer
moves out of coding—rather than the gain itself. The reallocation specifications therefore
carry an additional estimated parameter, 𝐴ˆ Auto .
    Table OA-1 reports the results. The four Cobb–Douglas rows report identical parame-
ters and losses: reallocation is irrelevant under Cobb–Douglas (Remark 5), and attenuation
data identify only the product 𝜃𝜇  ¯ (Remark 4), so all four cells re-estimate the same profile.
The Cobb–Douglas specifications are inconsistent with the curvature of the profile. Their
best fit has a loss more than 100 times that of the baseline CES specification (295.2 against
2.5, in units of 10−4 ), and the failure is the one Appendix B.1 anticipates: the attenuation
pattern in Figure 8 is too steep to be explained by a constant rate of decline.
    Adding decreasing returns to the CES specification improves the loss, from 2.5 to
1.2. However, this improvement comes with a set of parameters that are unstable and
implausible: the share runs to its boundary at 𝜃¯ = 0.981, and the returns to scale become
too small (𝜇 = 0.702). We therefore conclude that the CES specification with decreasing
returns to scale is not a good fit for the data. Even if it were the true model, however,
it would imply stronger complementarity than our baseline estimate (𝜎 = 0.128 against
0.229), reinforcing rather than weakening our main conclusion.


                                                     OA - 15
     The two reallocation specifications fit worse than their fixed-allocation counterparts,
despite the additional free parameter that reallocation introduces. The comparison is like-
for-like because every specification is fit to the same six layers. Under fixed allocation the
layer-1 term is degenerate, since 𝐴 is set to the observed lines gain; under reallocation it
is not, because the measured lines gain is itself an equilibrium object that the model must
                                   ˆ More importantly, they fit only by assuming a much
fit, and it is this term that pins 𝐴.
larger initial productivity shock than is observed: the estimated layer-1 gain is 𝐴ˆ = 41.05
under constant returns and 𝐴ˆ = 1,349 under decreasing returns, against a measured lines
gain of 3.34. Gains of this magnitude are not plausible for autocomplete. This suggests
that the model with reallocation is not a good fit to the estimated pattern, likely because
of the limited scope for reallocation in the short run.
    Taken together, the results in this appendix suggest that the main mechanism high-
lighted by our model survives in the extensions considered here. Moreover, the observed
attenuation pattern provides empirical support for the baseline specification used in the
paper.




                                           OA - 16
 C     Data Appendix
 This appendix describes the data underlying our empirical analysis. We first give an
 overview of the sample construction and then explain each step in detail.

 C.1     Overview

 Our sample consists of GitHub developers and their coding activity. It includes two groups
 of users: developers who adopted one of the AI coding tools we study (treated users) and
 an untreated control pool from which matched controls are drawn. The remainder of this
 appendix proceeds in two steps. First, we describe how we construct the set of treated and
 control developers. Second, we explain how we construct the outcome variables measuring
 these developers’ coding activity, aggregated into developer-week panels, including how
 we identify the activity of async coding agents.

 C.2     Coding Tool Usage Data

 We study seven different coding tools: five AI coding tools and two non-AI comparison
 tools (Docker and GitHub Pro), used as placebo and bounding exercises. The identification
 of the adoption of these tools comes from separate sources. For the GitHub Agent, OpenAI
 Codex, Claude Code, and Docker, we identify adoption from traces of activity on GitHub—
 dedicated bot usernames, branch-name prefixes, or specific configuration files. For GitHub
 Autocomplete, GitHub Sync, and GitHub Pro, we use internal data from Microsoft and
 identify adoption from users’ first activity or subscription to the product. We now provide
 the data collection details for each tool.

 C.2.1    GitHub Agent

 The GitHub Agent operates by creating pull requests that are authored by the bot account
 copilot-swe-agent[bot]. The commits within these pull requests are co-authored by the
 human developer who assigned the issue or requested the change.37
     To identify users, we use the GitHub REST API issues endpoints to retrieve all pull
 requests created by the GitHub Agent, identified by the author copilot-swe-agent[bot].
 We then use the GitHub REST API endpoint for listing commits on a pull request to retrieve
 all commits in each PR. For each pull request opened by the agent, we assign developer
 users according to the following priority hierarchy:

     1. Co-authors: We extract co-author information from commit messages by parsing
        the “Co-authored-by:” trailer, which follows the pattern Co-authored-by: Name

        <ID+login@users.noreply.github.com>. When multiple co-authors are identified
        within a single PR, we include all unique co-author IDs.

   2. Requested reviewers: For PRs without co-author information, we use the requested
      reviewer IDs from the pull request metadata.

   3. Assignees: For PRs with neither co-authors nor requested reviewers, we use the
      assignee IDs from the pull request metadata.

    We exclude the Copilot bot account itself from the user list. All but 1, 575 pull requests
(0.23% of the total) are linked to one or more human users according to the hierarchy
above; pull requests with no identifiable human user are excluded. We define the event
date as the creation date of the earliest associated Copilot pull request for each user.
   As of 2025-12-22, we retrieved 694,407 pull requests opened by the GitHub Agent and
2,084,472 commits associated with these pull requests. Applying the priority hierarchy
above, we identify 109,746 distinct users. Table OA-2 reports the user-source distribution,
showing that the majority of users are identified through the co-author field (65.08% of
pull requests and 58.64% of users) and that most of the remainder are identified through
requested reviewers, with assignees accounting for only a small share. Figure 3(c) shows
the distribution of event dates.

C.2.2    OpenAI Codex

We use the GitHub REST API endpoints for issues to retrieve all pull requests created by
Codex, identified by the head branch prefix head:codex/. We define a user as the creator
of a Codex pull request, as recorded in the API response’s user field. As of 2025-12-22,


                                                      OA - 18
 we retrieved 2,643,226 pull requests created by 142,902 Codex users. We exclude the
 very few pull requests (21 in total) with creation dates outside the feasible observation
 window—either before 2025-05-16 (the release date of Codex38) or after the data collection
 date. Figure 3(b) shows the distribution of event dates.

 C.2.3    Claude Code

 Unlike the asynchronous agents, sync agents leave no distinct trace of their coding activity
 in our data: their output is committed by the developer under their own account and
 is indistinguishable from code written without assistance. Claude Code, however, stores
 project-specific memory and instructions in a CLAUDE.md file, which it automatically pulls
 into its context when starting a conversation.39 Because this file is prominent in the
 workflow and tends to be created soon after a developer begins using the tool, we take the
 appearance of a CLAUDE.md file as an indicator of Claude Code adoption. Accordingly, we
 use the GitHub REST API code search endpoint (/search/code) to search for files named
 CLAUDE.md within repositories owned by users in our user universe.40
     For each discovered CLAUDE.md file, we use the GitHub REST API commits endpoint
 (/repos/{owner}/{repo}/commits?path={path}) to retrieve the complete commit history
 for that file. We define a user as a Claude user if they appear as an author of any CLAUDE.md
 commit, and we assign each such author’s earliest CLAUDE.md commit date as the event
 date.
    We identify 59,614 CLAUDE.md files across 51,865 repositories, along with 179,879 as-
 sociated commits authored by 28,558 unique authors. We exclude users whose event
 dates fall before 2025-02-24 (the release of Claude Code) or after the data collection date.
 Figure 3(a) shows the distribution of event dates.

 C.2.4    GitHub Sync

 We observe AI tool usage data for GitHub Copilot subscribers from Microsoft, covering
 April 2025 through December 2025. These data record usage at the level of the individual
 interaction—a prompt for the sync agent and a completion for autocomplete—together
 with the tool used, which allows us to identify the first time each developer uses the sync

C.2.5   GitHub Autocomplete

We obtain from Microsoft a universe of 2,985,745 individual subscribers to GitHub Copilot;
397,586 of these have subscription dates in 2022, spanning 2022-06-21 to 2022-12-26. At
the time, Copilot offered only autocomplete functionality, so we treat these subscribers as
autocomplete adopters. Unlike the sync agent, this dataset does not record actual usage,
so we take the subscription date as the adoption date.

     Similar to GitHub Copilot, we obtain a universe of individual subscribers to GitHub
