 to 2022-12-31. As with autocomplete, we do not observe usage in these data and take the
 subscription date as the adoption date.

 C.2.7   Docker

 Docker is a widely used open-source platform for containerization: it packages an appli-
 cation together with its dependencies and system configuration into a container that runs
 identically on any machine. Developers configure a container through a Dockerfile, a
 plain-text file committed to the repository that specifies the steps required to build the
 container image. The appearance of a Dockerfile in a repository therefore marks the
 point at which a developer begins using Docker for that project, in the same way that a
 CLAUDE.md file marks the adoption of Claude Code; Appendix E.3 discusses its use as a
 placebo and bounding exercise.
     We therefore study developers who commit Dockerfile files. We use the GitHub REST
 API code search endpoint (/search/code) with the query filename:dockerfile to search
 for files named “dockerfile” (case-insensitive) within repositories owned by users in our
 user universe. For each discovered Dockerfile, we retrieve the complete commit history
 and define a user as a Dockerfile user if they appear as a commit author. The event date
 is defined as the earliest Dockerfile commit date for each user.
     As of 2025-12-22, we identify 1,942,751 Dockerfile files across 1,073,370 repositories,
 with 5,257,726 associated commits authored by 540,709 unique users. The earliest and
 latest event dates in these data are 2018-01-01 and 2025-12-19, respectively.

 C.2.8   Sample Selection

 Table OA-3 lists, for each tool, the data source, the treated window over which adoptions
 enter the analysis, and the last adoption date. For the AI tools released in 2025, the treated
 window ends with our data collection in December 2025 and begins with the tool’s release,
 or, for GitHub Sync, with the start of reliable usage data (Table OA-3). We deliberately
 stop including adoptions after December 2025: control outcomes are measured one year
 earlier, so including 2026 adoptions would require drawing controls from 2025, and since
 the adoption of agentic AI tools became widespread from mid-2025 onward, we could no
 longer ensure that matched controls do not themselves use AI tools. For autocomplete,
41GitHub Docs—GitHub’s Plans


                                            OA - 22
we include only adoptions in 2022, after the product launched, to rule out contamination
from other AI tools, such as the use of ChatGPT for coding. For GitHub Pro, we likewise
use 2022 to rule out the use of AI tools. Docker adoption is included from 2022 through
2025 to give us enough statistical power.
    Table OA-4 then summarizes how the sample changes as we apply our sample selection
criteria. Panel A reports the number of users, and Panels B and C report the same
for commits and pull requests. (For Codex, these counts exclude the 21 pull requests
with creation dates outside the feasible observation window.) Each column applies the
selection criteria progressively. “Unconditional” corresponds to all users identified in
the raw adoption data. “In window” restricts the sample to adoptions within each tool’s
treated window, as described in Table OA-3. “With commit data” counts the users for
whom we download commit data; this sample differs from the previous column for a few
reasons: the account has since been deleted, the user has more than 100,000 commits in
the collection window, or the user has more than 1,000 commits on any single day. We
eliminate these accounts because such implausibly large volumes typically correspond to
bot activity, and their complete retrieval is infeasible under GitHub REST API pagination
and rate limits. “History floor” conditions on developers having activity at or before week
−11 relative to adoption; we impose this restriction because we do not want to analyze the
commit activity of users who joined the platform to try these tools, but rather condition
on already-active developers.
    “Active at adoption” drops users who are not active at the time of adoption. This
restriction (both a commit and a pull request in the adoption week) is imposed only on the
subscription-based tools, whose Microsoft adoption dates need not coincide with public
GitHub activity; for Claude Code, the async agents, and Docker, adoption is itself public
activity, and we do not impose the additional requirement (estimates are similar when
we do; Appendix Table OA-11). As expected, this significantly reduces the sample sizes
for GitHub Autocomplete, GitHub Sync, and GitHub Pro, since adoption of these tools
is identified off-platform and an adopter may have little or no public GitHub activity.
“After top-1% activity trim” drops the top 1% of adopters by pre-period commit volume,
removing extremely active users whose volumes, again, typically correspond to bots.
Finally, we match the remaining users to control developers, and “Matched” reports the
number of matched users. The matching procedure is described in Appendix D, and the
order in which these data were collected in Section C.5.
   Panels B and C report the corresponding volumes of commits and pull requests. These
panels start from the “With commit data” column, since activity volumes can be computed
only for the users whose commit data we download. Across the selection steps, the decline

primarily eliminate users who are not active on the platform. For autocomplete, for
example, the number of users falls from 336,451 to 15,328—a decline of roughly 95%—
but commits fall only from 25,021,718 to 4,957,828 (roughly 80%), and pull requests from
2,203,485 to 972,142 (roughly 56%). Our sample selection therefore eliminates a large
fraction of users while retaining a disproportionately large share of the coding activity.

C.3    Construction of the Control Pool

To match each treated user to a control, we draw a set of candidate users from a broad
universe of GitHub users. We next describe this procedure.
   We first enumerate all GitHub accounts. In August 2026, we walked the GitHub REST
API users endpoint (https://api.github.com/users?since=1&per_page=100) over the
entire account-id space, retrieving every existing account: 275,822,614 accounts in total, of
which 256,043,973 are user accounts and 11,253,874 are organizations, with the remainder
bot and mannequin accounts. We use this enumeration to characterize the universe of
GitHub accounts; the control pool itself is constructed from GHArchive, as described
below.
   Ideally, this entire universe would serve as the control pool. However, because we
match on commit activity, we would need each user’s commit history, and downloading
commit data for all 256 million user accounts is infeasible under GitHub API rate limits.
We instead use GHArchive (GHArchive), a complete public log of GitHub events that can
be downloaded in bulk and records every user’s push events. Measuring activity directly


                                                      OA - 24
from it is unreliable, however, especially toward the end of our sample period (Appendix
Figure OA-18), so we use GHArchive only as a screening tool to select a set of users for
whom we download commit data and which constitutes the control pool.
    We select users as follows. Since treated users are AI adopters, who tend to be active
developers, we first include all accounts with twenty or more push events over 2021–2025
(13,446,654 accounts); this captures a relatively active set of developers. To also represent
less active developers, we add a stratified random sample of the 54,487,779 accounts with
fewer than twenty pushes: within each exact push count 𝑛 = 0, . . . , 19, we draw a fixed
number of accounts, declining linearly from 900,000 accounts at 𝑛 = 0 to 100,000 at 𝑛 = 19
(10,000,000 accounts in total, drawn without replacement with a fixed seed). Because we
set the sampling rates ourselves (3.0% of zero-push accounts up to 27.6% of nineteen-
push accounts), population aggregates can be recovered with inverse-probability weights.
Finally, we resolve each selected account’s numeric id to its current login through the
REST API; 13,152,488 of the ≥20-push accounts and 9,490,361 of the sampled low-activity
accounts resolve, and the remainder are deleted or suspended. The combined control pool
contains 22,642,849 accounts.
   Table OA-5 traces this construction, reporting at each stage the number of users and,
where available, their corresponding commit and pull request volumes. We start from
the 67,934,433 accounts that appear as event actors in GHArchive over 2021–2025. Apply-
ing the selection procedure above, we select 23,446,654 accounts for collection, of which
22,642,849 resolve to a current login; the remainder are deleted or suspended accounts.
Of these, 20,795,563 have commit data: the gap reflects accounts with no commits over
2020–2026 as well as accounts at bot scale, which we exclude. Requiring at least one pull
request—the condition for entering a donor pool—leaves 8,211,358 users, of whom 434,982
distinct developers are ultimately matched to at least one treated adopter.

C.4   Construction of Outcome Variables

This section describes how we construct the outcome variables used in our analysis for
both treated and control developers. The variables fall into two groups: those used in the
matching process, which we call the commit list and the pull request list, and the outcomes
used in the event study. We refer to the commit and pull request data used in matching as
lists because they come from a different download process covering a different user pool;
the corresponding event-study outcomes are simply called commits and pull requests. We
go through each variable in turn and explain its data source.




                                          OA - 25
C.4.1   User-level Commit List

We use the GitHub REST API List commits endpoint and filter requests by the author
query parameter using each user’s GitHub login to retrieve commit data between January
2020 and May 2026, separately for the treated and control groups. This endpoint returns
commits on each repository’s default branch, so the developer-level series covers main-
branch activity only. Commits that an asynchronous agent makes on its own feature
branch are collected separately, from the agents’ pull requests (Section C.4.15), and are
therefore captured even though they do not appear in this list.
    In the commit lists we retrieve, the same commit can appear multiple times: when the
repository containing the original commit is forked, a single commit—identical in every
field, including its sha, except for the repository path—appears in the original repository
and in each of its forks, and would be counted once per copy absent deduplication. We
therefore deduplicate commits at the level of the commit sha, assigning each distinct sha
a single canonical repository and discarding every other copy. While this procedure need
not retain the repository in which the commit was originally made, the commit’s date is
preserved in the forked copies, so the choice does not affect the commit, line, or pull request
outcomes; it introduces some small error in the outcomes keyed to repository identity—
repositories touched, files touched (identified by repository–filename pairs), and releases,
which are counted on the repositories a user’s commits touch. Overall, 5.8% of unique
commits appear in more than one copy and are collapsed to a single record in this way.
   This procedure gives us the list of commits each user makes, together with their dates,
from which we construct the time series of commits. Note that this procedure does
not capture commits that users have since deleted; our evidence suggests, however, that
deletion is not significant (see Appendix E.5).

C.4.2   User-level Pull Request List

For the treated users identified in Section C.2 and the control pool constructed in Sec-
tion C.3, we also download the list of pull requests each user created. We use the
GitHub Search API to query all pull requests authored by each user’s login within the
collection window. For each pull request we retrieve its pull_request_url (of the form
.../repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{number}), the creating user’s user_id, and times-
tamps including created_at, merged_at, and state. We deduplicate pull requests by
their pull_request_url.




                                           OA - 26
C.4.3   Commits

We download the commit content only for the treated users whose adoption date falls
within the analysis window, as defined in Section C.2.8, and, among controls, only for
the matched control users. We impose this restriction because the commit endpoint
returns one commit per call, making this the most expensive step of our data collection
(Section C.5).
    We download the content of every commit in each user’s commit list. For each commit,
we follow its url (of the form .../repos/{owner}/{repo}/commits/{sha}) to the GitHub
REST API commit endpoint and retrieve the commit’s metadata: its line statistics (total
lines changed, additions, and deletions), the list of files it touches, and its commit message.
    For users of the GitHub Agent and Codex async agents, we distinguish between human-
authored and agent-authored commits, classifying commits as agent-authored when they
occur within agent-initiated pull requests. Although human developers can, in principle,
contribute commits to such pull requests, we observe that such cases are extremely rare.
The two sets are disjoint by construction (a commit is agent-authored if and only if it lies in
an agent-initiated pull request), and we use this decomposition to examine human coding
activity and AI-generated code separately (Section C.4.15).
   From each developer’s commit list—which records, per commit, the commit url, the
commit_author_date, and the author_id—we count the distinct commits (by sha, after
deduplication) whose author date falls in each week.

C.4.4   Lines of Code

We measure the total number of lines of code each developer writes in each week, using
the commit contents downloaded in Section C.4.3. Each commit’s record reports its line
statistics: the number of lines added (stats_additions), the number of lines deleted
(stats_deletions), and their sum (stats_total), where a modified line counts as one
addition and one deletion. To construct the weekly outcome, we sum stats_total across
a developer’s commits whose author date falls in each week.

C.4.5   Files Touched

We measure the number of distinct files each developer touches in each week, using the
same commit contents. Each commit’s record lists the files it changes with, for each file,
its filename, status, and per-file additions, deletions, and changes. We identify a file
by the pair (repository, filename) and, for the weekly outcome, count the distinct files
appearing in a developer’s commits within each week.


                                           OA - 27
C.4.6   Pull Requests

We construct a panel of pull requests at the developer level, distinguishing between
developer-initiated pull requests and agent-initiated pull requests for asynchronous agents:
we assign each pull request to a week by its created_at and count the pull requests a devel-
oper creates each week, classifying each as agent-initiated or developer-initiated according
to whether its pull_request_url appears in the Codex or Copilot agent pull-request lists.
As discussed above, we use agent-initiated pull requests to identify adoption of async
agents, and developer-initiated pull requests to measure developer activity over time.

C.4.7   Repositories Touched

We measure the number of distinct repositories touched by each developer in each week.
This allows us to get a sense of the extent to which developers are working on multiple
projects at once.
     We parse the repository {owner}/{repo} from each commit url, assign each commit to
its canonical repository after deduplication, and count the distinct repositories a developer
commits to within each week.

C.4.8   Releases

Repositories on GitHub can be ‘released’ by their owners, which typically involves tagging
a commit as a release, uploading related binaries, and posting a release note and/or
changelog. Releases let us gauge the extent to which the repositories a developer works
on are shipping versioned output to their users.
   We collect releases at the repository level. For each repository in our sample we query
the GitHub REST API releases endpoint (/repos/{owner}/{repo}/releases), retrieving
results 100 at a time and paging through the returned releases. For each release we obtain
the repository it belongs to, the date and time it was created, the user who authored it, its
tag and title, and whether it is marked as a draft or a pre-release; for our measure we use
only the repository and the creation date.
    To build a developer-level weekly outcome, we date each release by the week in which
it was created. We say a developer touches a repository in a given week if the developer
makes at least one commit to that repository during that week. For each developer and
each week, we take the set of repositories the developer touched in any of the twelve weeks
ending in that week and count how many distinct repositories in that set had at least one
release created during that week.




                                          OA - 28
C.4.9    Lines Added

We measure the total number of lines each developer adds in each week, summing
stats_additions (Section C.4.3) across the developer’s commits whose author date falls
in each week.

C.4.10    Lines Deleted

We measure the total number of lines each developer deletes in each week, summing
stats_deletions across the developer’s commits within each week.

C.4.11    Lines per Commit

We measure the average size of a developer’s commits in each week, dividing the weekly
sum of stats_total by the number of commits in that week. The outcome is defined only
for weeks with at least one commit.

C.4.12    Files per Commit

We measure the average number of distinct files per commit in each week: the weekly
average, across a developer’s commits, of the number of distinct files each commit touches.
The outcome is defined only for weeks with at least one commit.

C.4.13    Branches Created

We measure the number of branches each developer creates in each week. This outcome
comes from a separate collection: for each repository in our sample we query the GitHub
REST API activity endpoint (/repos/{owner}/{repo}/activity), paging through the
returned events by cursor; each event records its type, the branch it refers to, its timestamp,
and the acting user. We count, for each developer and week, the branch-creation events
in which the developer is the actor. The API retains no events before March 2023, so
this outcome exists only from 2023-03-07 onward, which is why it cannot be estimated for
autocomplete, whose adoptions all occur in 2022 (Appendix D.6).

C.4.14    Branches Deleted

We measure the number of branches each developer deletes in each week, counting the
branch-deletion events in which the developer is the actor. These events come from the
same GitHub REST API activity endpoint used for branch creations, and are subject to the
same March 2023 retention floor.




                                           OA - 29
C.4.15   Asynchronous Agent Outcomes

To estimate async effects separately, we need to tell the asynchronous agents’ activity apart
from the developer’s own. We do so for the two async agents we observe, the GitHub
Agent and Codex, constructing agent-specific versions of five outcomes (commits, lines
changed, files touched, repositories touched, and pull requests created): each is measured
on the activity inside the agent’s pull requests, with the developer’s own activity measured
outside them. Below we explain how agent activity is identified and attributed.

GitHub Agent The agent opens pull requests under its own bot account, copilot-swe-agent[bot].
We retrieve every pull request opened by this account, together with the commits each
one contains, and count a pull request and its commits as agent activity only from the
agent’s public-release date (2025-05-19) onward. Since the bot, not the developer, is the
committer of record, its commits must be reassigned to the human who initiated the work.
The Co-authored-by trailers in each bot commit message list the collaborating developers’
GitHub numeric identifiers; after excluding the agent’s own two bot accounts (numeric
identifiers 198982749 and 175728472), we create one record per named developer. Commits
whose message is exactly “Initial plan” or “Initial plan for issue” are the agent’s automated
planning commits, and we drop them. When a bot commit names no co-author at all, we
look to the other commits in the same pull request: if they point to exactly one developer,
the commit is assigned to that developer; otherwise it is dropped.

Codex For Codex, no reassignment is needed, since its commits are recorded under the
developer’s own account. We identify Codex pull requests by the codex/ prefix of their
head branch and count them as agent activity from Codex’s public-release date (2025-05-
16) onward.
   With agent activity identified and attributed, each outcome is computed three times
per developer-week: once on commits inside the developer’s GitHub-agent pull requests,
once on commits inside their Codex pull requests, and once on commits outside any agent
pull request. Pull requests created are split along the same lines into agent-initiated and
developer-initiated, so agent activity never enters the developer’s own totals.

C.5   Data Collection Procedure

This subsection summarizes the order in which we collect the data described above and the
reasoning behind that order. The binding constraint throughout is the GitHub REST API’s
rate limit: listing endpoints return up to one hundred commits or pull requests per call,
whereas metadata endpoints require one call per commit (for line counts and files changed)
and several calls per repository (for branches, releases, and activity). Collecting per-


                                          OA - 30
commit metadata for the full control universe would require billions of calls; collecting
it only for matched controls requires hundreds of millions. The pipeline is therefore
organized breadth-first: we first build cheap, complete user universes from bulk archives,
then collect activity lists for those universes, then match, and only then collect the expensive
per-commit and per-repository metadata, for the treated group in full and for the control
group only where a developer was actually matched. Figure OA-2 visualizes the flow; the
steps below give the details.

Step 1. Control pool from GHArchive. We download the complete GHArchive event
        stream for 2021–2025 and count push events for every account observed as an
        event actor (67,934,433 accounts in total). From these we build the control pool
        described in Appendix C.3: all 13,446,654 accounts with twenty or more pushes,
        plus a stratified sample of less active accounts, resolved to 22,642,849 current
        logins. We use GHArchive only for this screening step, not to measure activity,
        because its coverage of commit activity deteriorates after mid-2024.

Step 2. Treated users from adoption signals. The treated pool comes from the seven
        per-tool adoption signals described earlier in this appendix: Microsoft records for
        GitHub Autocomplete, GitHub Pro, and GitHub Sync; public GitHub traces for the
        GitHub Agent, Codex, Claude Code, and Docker. Together the signals identify
        3,540,554 distinct adopters, of whom 997,015 adopt within their tool’s analysis
        window (Table OA-3).

Step 3. Commit and pull request lists for both groups. For every user in both pools we
        retrieve the full commit list (commits-by-author endpoint) and pull request list
        over 2020-01-01 through 2026-05-31, excluding accounts at bot scale (more than
        100,000 commits in total or 1,000 in a day), and deduplicate commits at the sha
        level because forks copy commit histories verbatim. The lists contain 2,662,776,922
        unique control commits, 200,647,903 control pull requests by 8,474,057 users,
        388,180,133 treated commits, and 46,871,805 treated pull requests. Treated users
        already covered by the control scrape are not re-fetched.

Step 4. Matching. Each in-window treated developer is then matched to a control de-
        veloper (donor-pool eligibility and the matching procedure are described in Ap-
        pendix D). This yields 489,396 matched pairs across the seven tools, built from
        456,220 distinct treated developers and 434,982 distinct controls: matching is one-
        to-one and without replacement within a tool, but nothing prevents a control from
        being matched to adopters of several tools. Table OA-4 traces the treated sample
        tool by tool, from identified adopters down to the final estimation sample.

                                           OA - 31
                                 Figure OA-2: Data Collection Flow

                         Control track                                           Treated track

             GHArchive event stream 2021–2025                           Adoption signals (7 tools)
                67,934,433 actor accounts                        Microsoft records + public GitHub traces


         Control pool: all 13,446,654 with ≥20 pushes                         Treated users
 + stratified <20 sample (10,000,000) → 22,642,849 resolved      3,540,554 adopters → 997,015 in window


               Commit & PR lists (REST API)                           Commit & PR lists (REST API)
          2,662,776,922 commits · 200,647,903 PRs                  388,180,133 commits · 46,871,805 PRs


                                              Matching (Appendix D)
                                    1:1 nearest neighbor, one-year-shifted week
                                               489,396 matched pairs


             Per-commit & per-repo metadata                          Per-commit & per-repo metadata
                    matched controls only                                   all in-window treated
       344,060,931 commit records · 11,536,060 repos           385,135,261 commit records · 14,735,025 repos


                                               Developer-week panels
                                    997,015 treated · 658,960 control × 334 weeks

                                               Matched event studies

Notes: The pipeline runs breadth-first. The control track (left) screens the full GitHub population through
GHArchive push counts and collects commit and pull request lists for the resulting universe; the treated track
(right) does the same for adopters identified from the seven tool signals. Matching operates on these lists. The
expensive per-commit and per-repository metadata (dashed boxes) are then collected for all in-window treated
developers but only for matched controls, which reduces the control-side metadata collection from roughly 2.7
billion API calls to 346,457,054.


Step 5. Per-commit metadata. Commit-level metadata (lines added and deleted, files
        changed, and the commit message) costs one API call per commit, which is
        why matching comes first. On the treated side we fetch all 388,180,133 com-
        mits (385,135,261 retrieved, a coverage of 99.2%); on the control side we fetch only
        the 346,457,054 commits belonging to matched controls (344,060,931 retrieved,
        99.3%). Fetching metadata for the full control universe instead would have taken
        roughly 2.7 billion calls.

Step 6. Repository metadata. Every repository appearing in the collected commits (14,735,025
        on the treated side, 11,536,060 on the control side) gets a further set of pulls: the
        repository record (creation date, default branch), the branch list, the release his-
        tory, the activity feed (available from March 2023 onward), merge commits, and

                                                   OA - 32
        the pull request behind each merge event. The release and branch outcomes in the
        developer-week panels are built from these inputs.

Step 7. Asynchronous agent footprint. Separately from the user-level collection, we
        download the async agents’ own traces: every pull request opened by the GitHub
        Agent’s bot account or on a codex/ branch (6,551,030 pull requests), the com-
        mits inside each such pull request (14,554,797 distinct commits), their per-commit
        metadata, and, for the GitHub Agent, the pull request objects needed to attribute
        each agent PR to a human developer (1,840,294 attributed). These data let us
        separate agent-generated from human-generated output.

Step 8. Panels. Finally, we aggregate all collected activity into the developer-week panels
        described above: 997,015 treated and 658,960 control developers over 334 weeks.

   Because matching precedes the control-side metadata collection, a change to the match-
ing specification requires re-collecting metadata only for newly matched controls; the
commit and PR lists of Step 3 cover the full universes and never need to be re-fetched.

C.6   GitHub Aggregate Data

This section describes the data behind the aggregate GitHub series of Figure 10. All three
series are monthly and cover public GitHub repositories.

Claude Code Commit Share The numerator is the daily count of public commits au-
thored or co-authored by Claude Code, identified from the commit signature the tool
adds by default, as tracked by claudescode.dev; we aggregate the daily series to calendar
months. The denominator is the total number of public commits per month, obtained
from the GitHub Search API commit endpoint. The Search API reports estimated counts
from a sampled index rather than exact totals, so the share is approximate. As discussed in
the main text, it is in any case likely an undercount of AI-assisted activity, since developers
often commit AI-generated work under their own name.

Pull Requests Merged Monthly counts of merged pull requests on public repositories,
obtained from the GitHub Search API issues endpoint with one query per calendar month
restricting to pull requests merged in that month.

New Repositories Created Monthly counts of newly created public repositories, ob-
tained from the GitHub Search API repository endpoint with one query per calendar
month on the repository creation date; repository search excludes forks by default, so the
series counts non-fork repositories.


                                           OA - 33
 C.7     Software Marketplace Data

 We collect data on four software marketplaces: the Google Play Store (Android), the Apple
 App Store (iOS), the Chrome Web Store, and SourceForge. For each marketplace we mea-
 sure the number of apps available over time and an app-level measure of usage; the usage
 measure differs across stores. For each marketplace we obtain a near-complete catalog
 at regular intervals and, from the resulting sequence of snapshots, build a longitudinal
 app-level dataset that records each product’s first and last observed dates, the months in
 which it appears, and the values of a set of marketplace-specific attributes. Because apps
 are periodically removed from the store, a single current snapshot’s creation dates would
 understate entry; counting new apps therefore requires the full sequence of historical
 snapshots.
     The raw data extend to our collection in August 2026 and are thus nearly real-time. In
 every panel, we restrict the usage analysis to entry cohorts from 2020-01 through 2026-05,
 so that each cohort’s three-month usage window is fully observed; each app contributes
 one row per calendar month it is observed. Apps sometimes drop out of and reappear in
 the catalog between an app’s first and last observed snapshot; for those interim months we
 back-fill attributes from the most recent prior snapshot. The remainder of this subsection
 describes each marketplace in turn.

 C.7.1    Google Play Store (Android)

 Our Google Play data come from SimilarWeb (previously known as 42matters), which
 crawls public store pages on a fixed schedule; we license its weekly snapshots, each a flat
 record per app per crawl.42 The snapshots we obtain begin on 2020-02-29 and run through
 2026-07-25 (335 in total). Coverage is worldwide in principle, but an app enters a given
 snapshot only if the crawl actually reached it that week.
     The Play Store package name serves as our persistent app identifier; each record also
 names the publishing developer account and reports the app’s category and whether it is an
 Application or a Game. Installs are the one awkward variable: Google discloses them only
 as ranges (“1,000–5,000”, “5,000–10,000”, and so on up to “10,000,000,000–50,000,000,000”),
 so we store both bounds and treat the lower one as a conservative measure of cumulative
 installs; most of our cohort-level summaries use it. Beyond that, a record carries a running
 version count, an indicator for an update since the prior snapshot, the average rating on
 the 1–5 scale, and the total number of ratings received.
     An app’s entry and exit dates are the earliest and latest snapshots in which its package
42See similarweb.com.



                                           OA - 34
 name appears; the resulting file is our universe of Play Store apps over the sample window.
 To build the monthly panel, we keep, for each app and calendar month, the record from the
 last weekly crawl of that month (so monthly variables always come from the same point
 within the month) and fill interim months in which an app temporarily leaves the store by
 carrying its most recent prior snapshot forward. The panel has 250,922,156 app–month
 rows for 9,815,182 unique Android apps.
    Three limitations are worth keeping in mind: the install ranges make apps within a
 bucket indistinguishable; a weekly crawl can miss an app delisted and re-listed within the
 same week; and we observe only the worldwide catalog, not country-level availability.

 C.7.2   Apple App Store (iOS)

 We license weekly snapshots of the Apple App Store from the same vendor (SimilarWeb),
 spanning 2020-03-01 through 2026-07-26 across 334 weekly crawls. The data are collected
 in the same way as the Android snapshots, one record per app per snapshot.
     Each record identifies the app by its App Store track identifier, our persistent id, and
 by the developer account that publishes it, and reports the primary genre and category,
 the content-advisory rating, the current price, an in-app-purchase indicator, the binary’s
 size in bytes, and iPhone and iPad compatibility. The raw data list every country store in
 which the app is available; we retain the primary country, the number of countries, and
 an indicator for the U.S. store, together with counts of supported languages, supported
 devices, and distinct in-app features. Rating signals cover the lifetime average rating and
 total count, the same figures for the current version, and the one-to-five-star breakdown;
 we also record a per-snapshot update indicator and a running version count. Apple does
 not publicly disclose download or install counts, so the iOS panel contains no download
 variable.
    The monthly panel is built exactly as for Android and contains 155,959,418 app–month
 rows covering 5,287,204 unique iOS apps. As on Android, weekly snapshots can miss
 short-lived listings; because Apple discloses no install counts, we measure adoption
 through ratings.

 C.7.3   Chrome Web Store

 We license daily snapshots of the Chrome Web Store from chrome-stats.com, a data
 provider that crawls the public Chrome Web Store on a fixed schedule.43 We use two
 of its feeds. The ranking-stats feed is a daily snapshot of every item available in the store
 on the crawl date, with the item’s full catalog metadata attached (publisher, category,
43See chrome-stats.com.


                                           OA - 35
version, ratings, current user count, and the item-type label). The all-versions feed records
each item’s version-release history: every version ever published, with its release date.
The all-versions feed carries no metadata beyond name, author, size, and description. Its
release dates, however, extend back to the launch of the Chrome Web Store in 2009, far
earlier than our crawl coverage of ranking-stats, which begins on 2023-03-31.
    Combining the two feeds gives each item an entry and an exit date: entry is the earlier
of the first release date in all-versions and the first appearance in ranking-stats, and exit
is the last appearance in ranking-stats or, for items never observed there, the most recent
release date in all-versions. All metadata fields (ratings, user count, category, publisher,
etc.) come from ranking-stats and are therefore available only from 2023-03-31 onward; for
the months before that, items have entry information from all-versions but no measured
attributes.
    Each ranking-stats record identifies the item by its Chrome Web Store id, our persistent
identifier, and reports its type (extension, app, or theme), its category (e.g., Productivity,
Developer Tools), the publishing developer account, the current user count, the average
rating and rating count, a running version count, and an update indicator.
   Apps have been deprecated by Google and no longer receive updated values, and
themes are visual customizations rather than software, so we restrict the analysis to exten-
sions.
      Unlike Google Play, which reports a cumulative installation range, the Chrome Web
Store reports a current user count at the time of each snapshot, which can move both up
and down as users add or remove the extension. The reported count is exact for small
extensions but rounded to bucket boundaries (10,000, 20,000, 30,000, . . . , 100,000, 200,000,
. . . , 1,000,000, 2,000,000, and so on) for larger ones. To construct a measure comparable
to the other marketplaces, we treat the running maximum of the per-snapshot user count
over the observation window as cumulative installs.
    The monthly panel is built as for Android and iOS, applied to the combined entry and
exit dates above: one row per extension per observed month, pegged to the last ranking-
stats snapshot of that month, with interim gaps back-filled from the most recent prior
snapshot.
  The main Chrome-specific limitation is the bucket rounding described above, which
makes two large extensions in the same bucket indistinguishable.

C.7.4   SourceForge

We scrape the SourceForge project directory ourselves. The list of projects comes from
SourceForge’s published sitemap feeds at https://sourceforge.net/sitemap-N.xml,

                                           OA - 36
which yield 328,614 distinct projects. For each project we then fetch the monthly down-
load statistics from SourceForge’s public JSON statistics endpoint over the window 2020-01
through 2026-08. We scrape the project’s HTML overview page for its category and tag
assignments, summary description, listed license, registration date, and publicly visible
maintainer information. Finally, we pull the project’s release RSS feed to obtain the dated
history of file releases.
   For each project we observe a stable slug, used as the persistent identifier, and the
monthly download series (80 months). Metadata (category, tags, license, description,
registration date) are fixed attributes, and the release feed gives one row per release with
date, tag, and filename.
   Download counts are reported at the project (not file) level and capture downloads
from any SourceForge mirror, but exclude external mirrors and repackagings. The HTML
metadata reflects the state of the project page at scrape time rather than a historical record,
and the project enumeration is a single cross-section, so projects delisted from the sitemap
before the scrape do not appear in the panel.




                                           OA - 37
 D     Estimation Details
 D.1     Overview

 This appendix describes the estimation procedure underlying the matched difference-
 in-differences event studies in the main text. Section D.2 explains how we determine
 eligible control developers and form matched treated–control pairs; the remaining subsec-
 tions define the estimation sample (Section D.3) and set out the difference-in-differences
 specifications, along with the inference and winsorization conventions used throughout,
 together with the constructions behind the remaining exhibits and the production-function
 calibration.

 D.2     Matching Strategy

 D.2.1    Design

 For each tool, we pair every treated developer 𝑖 with a control developer 𝑗 observed one
 year earlier. We shift the adopter’s adoption week, denoted 𝑡 𝑖∗ , back one calendar year; the
                          𝑝
 shifted week, denoted 𝑡 𝑖 , is the control’s pseudo-adoption week, and we select as the match
                                                   𝑝
 a control developer 𝑗 whose activity around 𝑡 𝑖 resembles the adopter’s activity around
 adoption (Sections D.2.2 and D.2.3). In the estimation, the control’s outcomes are aligned in
 event time so that the pseudo-adoption week plays the role of the adoption week.44 Because
 the shift is exactly one year, seasonal patterns in developer activity (holidays, academic
 calendars, release cycles) stay balanced between the two groups. This matters because
 adoption dates for most tools span many months over which activity varies systematically.
 The per-tool treated and control windows are reported in Table OA-3.
     To avoid activity bias, we would like both sides of a match to be actively coding at
 the (pseudo-)adoption week. To ensure this, we require every matched control to have
 at least one commit and at least one pull request in the pseudo-adoption week. Treated
 developers face the same requirement at adoption, except for the tools whose adoption
 we observe on public GitHub. For these the adoption event itself guarantees some public
 activity in the adoption week (a commit for Claude Code and Docker, a pull request for
 Codex, and, for the GitHub Agent, a pull request opened by the agent on the developer’s
 behalf), and we impose no further requirement. Appendix Table OA-11 re-estimates the
 event studies imposing the full commit-and-pull-request requirement on all adopters,
 with similar results. The requirement therefore binds only for the tools identified from
44Weeks are the fixed seven-day blocks of the panel grid, anchored at 2020-01-01 (Appendix C). The adoption
  date is shifted back one calendar year (February 29 maps to March 1), and the pseudo-adoption week is the
  grid week containing the shifted date.



                                                 OA - 38
 Microsoft records (GitHub Autocomplete, GitHub Pro, and GitHub Sync), whose adoption
 dates need not coincide with any public GitHub activity. Table OA-4 traces the resulting
 counts stage by stage.

 D.2.2    Control Pool Eligibility

 We start from the control pool constructed in Appendix C.3 (Table OA-5). A candidate
 control is eligible for a given adopter if it (i) never adopts the tool in question during its
 treated window (Table OA-3); (ii) has both a commit and a pull request in the pseudo-
 adoption week, the activity requirement of Section D.2.1; and (iii) has at least one week
 with positive commits at or before event week −11 relative to the pseudo-adoption week,
 mirroring the requirement imposed on treated adopters.

 D.2.3    Matched-Pair Algorithm

 We pair treated and control developers with a sequential greedy algorithm, processing
 treated developers in random order. For each treated developer 𝑖:
                                                                                                𝑝
    1. Retrieve the set of eligible controls C𝑖 at the pseudo-adoption week 𝑡 𝑖 (Section D.2.2),
       removing any control already matched.

    2. For each 𝑗 ∈ C𝑖 , compute the absolute difference in pre-period commits Δ𝑖𝑗 =
       PreCommits𝑖 − PreCommits 𝑗 , where

                                 −1
                                 Õ                                                   −1
                                                                                     Õ
              PreCommits𝑖 =              commits   𝑖, 𝑡 𝑖∗ +𝑘   ,   PreCommits 𝑗 =           commits 𝑗, 𝑡 𝑝 +𝑘 ,
                                                                                                           𝑖
                                 𝑘=−11                                               𝑘=−11

         and select the control with the smallest difference.

    3. If several controls tie, one is chosen at random.45

     Because many eligible controls share a treated developer’s pre-period commit count,
 the realized difference Δ𝑖𝑗 ∗ is exactly zero for 99.8–100% of matched pairs. Matching is
 one-to-one and without replacement within each tool; a control developer may be matched
 in several tools’ samples.
    In practice, sequential matching never exhausts a donor pool: a treated developer goes
                                                     𝑝
 unmatched only when no eligible control exists at 𝑡 𝑖 in the first place—10,486 of 499,882
 adopters across the seven tools, mostly GitHub Pro (276 of 1,326 eligible adopters) and
 Docker (9,712 of 275,977).
45Both random orderings, the processing order over treated developers and the tie-breaking order, use fixed
  seeds, recorded for replication.


                                                   OA - 39
 D.3    Construction of the Estimation Sample

 Before estimation, we restrict the developer-week panel constructed in Appendix C in
 three steps, which together determine which developer-weeks enter the regressions and
 how weeks without recorded activity are treated: an active-span rule, an event-time grid,
 and a set of pair-level requirements. All are applied identically to treated and matched
 control developers.

 Active Span On GitHub we observe each account’s creation date, but accounts are rarely
 deleted: holding an account does not imply active use of the platform, and developers
 commonly leave without closing their accounts. To construct windows that reflect when
 a developer is active, we define each developer’s active span as the interval between their
 first and last week with strictly positive commits, computed on the full 2020–2026 panel.
 Weeks outside the span are excluded.
     Our active span can yield false positives due to natural gaps in activity that run into
 the end of the sample: a sporadically active developer’s final weeks would be classified as
 inactive even if they remained on the platform. We therefore drop the final six calendar
 weeks of the panel after computing the active span; the trim is uniform across tools and
 applied to treated and control developers alike. Beyond this we impose no balanced-panel
 requirement: late adopters simply contribute fewer post-adoption weeks, and the number
 of pairs underlying each event-time coefficient is reported alongside the estimates.

 Event-Time Grid Because controls are anchored one year before their matched adopter,
 treated and control developers cover different stretches of calendar time and can differ in
 data availability (for example, controls, starting a year earlier, have more post-period data).
 We therefore construct observations in event time: for each treated and control account,
 the estimation panel spans weeks −52 to +52 around its own anchor: the adoption week
                                                               𝑝
 𝑡 𝑖∗ for treated developers and the pseudo-adoption week 𝑡 𝑖 for controls.

 Pair-Level Requirements Two further rules operate at the pair level. First, both members
 of a pair must be observed at event week −1, the reference week. Second, since our main
 specification divides by pre-adoption activity to obtain percent effects, pairs in which
 either the treated or the control developer has no activity in the outcome variable over
 weeks −11 to −1 are dropped. 46
46This same selection is applied to the level specification as well—even though it does not divide by the
  individual pre-period mean—so that the two specifications are estimated on the same sample.




                                                OA - 40
D.4   Event Study Specification

For every outcome we estimate the same fixed-effects event study on the matched pairs.

                                                                              𝑝
control, aligned so that the control’s week 0 is its pseudo-adoption week 𝑡 𝑖 (Appendix D.2).

Normalization To facilitate cross-developer comparison and address heterogeneity in
baseline activity, we normalize the treated and the control outcome by their own pre-
period means:

where 𝑡 𝑖∗ denotes the treatment event week for developer 𝑖, 1{·} is the indicator function,
and 𝛼 𝑖 captures matched-pair fixed effects. The regression is estimated on the full −52
to +52 event grid of Appendix D.3; the indicators 𝛾𝑝𝑟𝑒 and 𝛾𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡 absorb the event weeks
outside the −11 to +30 coefficient window, and the base period is 𝑘 = −1. Standard errors
are clustered at the pair level (equivalently, the treated developer). Coefficients are read
as changes relative to the pre-period baseline (𝛽 𝑘 = 0.5 indicates a 50% increase relative
to the pre-period average). Because controls are observed one calendar year before their
treated partner, calendar-time shocks are not differenced out; the identifying assumptions
of this time-shifted design are discussed in Appendix F.

De-meaning Adjustment We re-center the estimated coefficient path on the pre-period
by subtracting the average of the pre-period coefficients (with the omitted 𝛽ˆ −1 entering as
zero) from every coefficient:
                                                                −1
                                                            1   Õ
                                             𝛽˜ 𝑘 = 𝛽ˆ 𝑘 −              𝛽ˆ 𝑗 .                                   (33)
                                                           11
                                                                𝑗=−11

This shifts the entire path by a constant, so week-to-week differences are unchanged;
post-period coefficients are read relative to the average pre-adoption level (the same base-

                                                     OA - 41
line as the binned specification below), and the pre-period coefficients average zero by
construction.

Post-Period Binned Regression To summarize treatment effects and facilitate compari-
son across tools, we estimate a binned specification that groups post-period weeks:

         𝑌     e𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑙 =
         e𝑖𝑡 − 𝑌            𝛽 0 · 1{𝑡 − 𝑡 𝑖∗ = 0}
                𝑖𝑡
                            + 𝛽1-10 · 1{1 ≤ 𝑡 − 𝑡 𝑖∗ ≤ 10}
                            + 𝛽11-20 · 1{11 ≤ 𝑡 − 𝑡 𝑖∗ ≤ 20}
                            + 𝛽21-30 · 1{21 ≤ 𝑡 − 𝑡 𝑖∗ ≤ 30}
                            + 𝛾𝑝𝑟𝑒 · 1{𝑡 < 𝑡 𝑖∗ − 11} + 𝛾𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡 · 1{𝑡 > 𝑡 𝑖∗ + 30} + 𝛼 𝑖 + 𝜀𝑖𝑡   (34)

The omitted category is the pre-period (weeks −11 to −1), so the binned coefficients need
no de-meaning: 𝛽 1-10 is the average treatment effect during weeks 1–10 relative to the pre-
period baseline, and similarly for the other bins. Fixed effects, clustering, and sample are as
in the weekly specification; the asynchronous outcomes use the same binned specification.

Pooled Specification For pooled analysis across coding agents, we stack the per-tool
panels, so the unit of observation becomes the developer-by-tool pair. The sync pool stacks
the four sync-capable tools (Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Agent, and GitHub Sync), with
commits measured outside agent pull requests for the two async tools so that the pooled
outcome captures the developers’ own activity. The async pool stacks the two asynchronous
agents (Codex and GitHub Agent) on the in-pull-request outcomes. Each developer-by-
tool unit receives its own matched-pair fixed effect, so a developer who adopts several tools
appears once per adoption and is treated as an independent unit (about 9% of sync-pool
units and 6% of async-pool units arise from such multiple adoptions). Standard errors are
clustered at the developer-by-tool level.

Winsorization and Estimation We winsorize dependent variables at the 1% level. For
the matched difference specifications, the treated-minus-control difference is winsorized
at both tails (1st and 99th percentiles), with the level and normalized outcomes winsorized
on their own separate distributions; percentiles are computed over the full −52 to +52
event grid, after the sample rules of Appendix D.3 are applied. For the asynchronous
outcomes we winsorize at the upper tail only (99th percentile), computed over positive
values in event weeks 0 and later. All regressions are estimated using the fixest package
in R.

D.5    Heterogeneity by Pre-Period Activity

To examine how treatment effects vary with baseline developer activity, we estimate a
quartile interaction specification. Each pair is assigned to a quartile 𝑞 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 4} of

 the average of the treated and control normalization denominators from Equation (31).47
 Averaging the two sides matters: if pairs were sorted on the treated developer’s mean
 alone, developers in the top quartile would be there partly because of positive noise in that
 mean and would regress toward it after adoption, while their controls, not selected on this
 noise, would not; the resulting reversion would contaminate the quartile-specific treatment
 effects. Averaging makes the selection symmetric in the two sides, so mean reversion affects
 treated and control outcomes alike and drops out of the difference. Quartile breakpoints

 D.6    Other Event-Study Specifications

 The remaining empirical results in the paper use specifications beyond those above; we
 document each here.

 Calendar-Time Effects (Figure 5) Each panel is the matched normalized event study of
 Equation (32), estimated on the subset of developers who adopted within the first three
 weeks of the tool’s release (for Claude Code, its general-availability date of May 22, 2025;
 for the other tools, the release dates of Appendix C), together with their matched controls.
 Within this fixed cohort, event time maps directly onto calendar time, so the horizontal
 axis can be labelled by calendar date; the specification, sample rules, and inference are
 otherwise unchanged.
47For the asynchronous outcomes, where the control series is identically zero, we use 𝑀 ¯ 𝑝𝑟𝑒 = 𝑌¯ 𝑝𝑟𝑒 , the
                                                                                          𝑖       𝑖
  developer’s pre-period mean of the corresponding overall outcome, so pairs are sorted by baseline total
  activity.




                                                    OA - 44
Mechanism Outcomes (Table 7, Panel A) Panel A reports the same normalized post-
period binned estimate (weeks 21–30 against the −11 to −1 baseline) applied to six mech-
anism outcomes. Lines per commit and files per commit are weekly averages per commit;
because they are undefined in weeks without commits, these two outcomes are estimated
conditional on weeks with at least one commit. Lines added, lines deleted, and branches
created and deleted are weekly counts. Additions and deletions are estimated on a com-
mon sample, as are branches created and deleted: in each case we take the intersection
of pairs passing the positive pre-period-mean rule for both outcomes, so the two columns
describe the same developers. Branch outcomes derive from data available only from
March 2023 onward; since every autocomplete adoption occurs in 2022, the autocomplete
branch cells have no estimation sample and are not reported.

Repository Abandonment (Table 7, Panel B) The abandonment series is computed from
the raw records of every repository that a treated developer contributed to, not from the
matched panel. We use a time series rather than an event study because abandonment is
a one-time, repository-level event rather than a weekly developer outcome; a developer-
week event study has little power against it. A repository counts as abandoned when the
last push it ever receives falls within one month of its creation. For each calendar year
2021–2026 we report the share of repositories created in that year that are abandoned; the
standard error is that of the mean of the year’s monthly abandonment shares. The series
starts in 2021 because push timestamps are unreliable before August 2020, and 2026 covers
repositories created in January through March, so that every repository is observed for at
least three months beyond its first month before the July 2026 data snapshot.

Release Size and Comment Share (Table 7, Panel B) The release statistics are computed
from a random draw of one million releases of repositories in our sample universe, of
which 916,071 stable releases (excluding drafts and pre-releases) enter the sample. We
sample because each statistic requires downloading the release’s full source archive; doing
this for every release is prohibitively costly in download time and storage. For each release
we download the source archive, keep code files only, and classify every line as blank,
comment, or code using extension-specific comment grammars. For each calendar year
we report the median lines and files per release, with bootstrap standard errors from
300 release-level resamples, and the comment share (total comment lines over total lines,
pooled across the year’s releases), with a bootstrap standard error computed the same way.
The 2026 figures cover January through March.




                                          OA - 45
D.7   Aggregate Series

GitHub Aggregate Series (Figure 10) The aggregate GitHub results are descriptive
monthly series (Appendix C.6); no regression is estimated. The construction of each
panel is described in the note to Figure 10; the rolling averages are three-month centered,
and the pre-2025 trend is a linear fit on the months through December 2024.

Marketplace Cohorts (Figure 11) The marketplace results are likewise descriptive series
built from the monthly app panels of Appendix C.7; no regression is estimated. The
cohort definition, usage measures, and usage-bin thresholds are given in Section 7 and the
note to Figure 11. We clarify three additional details. Late-arriving incumbents, which
we exclude from the cohorts, are apps entering the catalog more than three months after
their store release date, whose first-seen month hence reflects catalog arrival rather than
genuine entry. The cohort-usage series stops three months before the end of the sample, so
that every cohort’s three-month usage window is complete. All rolling averages, including
those applied to the bottom-row bin shares, are three-month centered.

D.8   Production Function Calibration

We calibrate the structural parameters (𝜃, 𝜎) to the empirical attenuation pattern for auto-
complete, which enters the hierarchy only at layer 1 (Section 6.3); this subsection states the
procedure formally. We assume 𝜃 and 𝜎 are constant across layers.
   Let 𝑔ˆ 𝑠 denote the empirical productivity gain at layer 𝑠 for autocomplete, in percent,
and let 𝐺ˆ 𝑠 = 1+ 𝑔ˆ 𝑠 /100 denote the corresponding gross gain. We set 𝐴 = 𝐺ˆ 1 and recursively
define the predicted gross gain at higher layers by composing the single-layer pass-through
formula in (8):

         𝐺 𝑠 (𝜃, 𝜎) = 𝜃 𝐺 𝑠−1 (𝜃, 𝜎)𝜌 + (1 − 𝜃)               ,     𝜌=       ,          𝑠 = 2, . . . , 𝑆.

We then choose (𝜃, 𝜎) to minimize the sum of squared log differences between predicted
and empirical gross gains across all six layers:

Under fixed allocation the 𝑠 = 1 term is identically zero, because 𝐴 is set to the observed
lines gain; the baseline calibration is therefore equivalently a sum over layers 2 through
𝑆. This is not so under reallocation, where the layer-1 gain is itself a fitted moment
(Appendix B.5).
