   Finally, we predict the attenuation pattern for sync and async agents by taking each
tool’s empirical lines-of-code effect as 𝐴 and applying the same recursion with the cali-
brated parameters (Figure 9); Section 6.3 discusses the interpretation of these counterfac-
tual predictions.




                                         OA - 47
 E     Robustness Checks
 In this appendix, we present the full set of robustness checks summarized in Section 5.5.
 Our analysis relies on observational data, so these checks are intended to support a causal
 interpretation of our estimates.

 E.1   Robustness to Matching

 Our baseline design matches each treated developer to a control developer with at least one
 commit and one pull request at the pseudo-adoption week, one year before the treated de-
 veloper’s adoption date, selecting the donor closest in pre-period commits (Appendix D.2).
 In this section we consider robustness to this procedure along four dimensions: the timing
 of the control group, the activity requirements imposed at the (pseudo-)adoption week,
 matching on additional observables, and the pre-period window used for matching. The
 results are reported in Tables OA-11 and OA-12 and Figure OA-11. We run these variants
 for commits and pull requests only, since retrieving the remaining outcomes for the full
 control pool is prohibitively costly.

 Contemporaneous Controls Instead of drawing controls from one year before adop-
 tion, we re-match each treated developer to a non-adopter satisfying the same activity
 requirements at the adoption week itself. The results are reported in Table OA-11 and
 Figure OA-11: the estimates are smaller—consistent with contemporaneous controls being
 partially treated from private (i.e., non-observable) adoption of AI tools, the concern that
 motivated the lagged design—but remain large and precisely estimated.

 Adoption-Week Activity Requirements In the baseline, control developers must have
 both a commit and a pull request in the (pseudo-)adoption week for every tool, while
 treated developers face this requirement only for the tools identified from Microsoft records
 (Appendix D.2). We re-match under uniform activity requirements, requiring a commit
 only, a pull request only, or both, enforced identically for every tool. 48 The results
 are reported in Table OA-11 and Figure OA-11 and are broadly similar: the pooled sync
 estimates are stable across gates, while the autocomplete estimate is somewhat sensitive
 to the commit-only gate.

 Additional Observables We additionally require the matched control to share the treated
 developer’s account-creation year. We do not impose this requirement in the baseline
 because it lowers the matching rate. The results are reported in Table OA-11 and Figure OA-
 11 and are very close to the baseline.
48As expected, the uniform commit-and-pull-request variant reproduces the paper specification exactly for
  the tools whose baseline already imposes both requirements.


                                                OA - 48
Matching Window The baseline matches on pre-period activity over the eleven weeks
before the (pseudo-)adoption week and normalizes by the same window. We condition on
this relatively short window because it improves match quality and because it controls for
the activity spell around adoption (the burst of engagement that coincides with adopting
a new tool), which is precisely the confound our design targets; matching on a longer pre-
period would instead control for a developer’s longer-run activity level. We nevertheless
re-match and re-normalize with pre-period windows beginning −22, −32, −42, and −52
weeks before adoption; the normalization window moves with the matching window, as
does the requirement of at least one week with positive commits at or before the window’s
start. Table OA-12 reports the results, which are broadly similar to the baseline. The
async pull-request estimates rise with the window length, but this is mechanical rather
than evidence of a larger effect: the interrupted-time-series outcome is normalized by
the developer’s pre-period mean, which declines as the window extends into less active
periods.

E.2   Robustness to Empirical Specification

Our headline estimates report the average percentage treatment effect, scaling each de-
veloper’s outcome by their pre-period mean before averaging across developers. In this
section we consider robustness to this specification along four dimensions: estimating in
levels rather than in normalized outcomes, tightening the winsorization threshold, remov-
ing the activity-window restriction, and estimating on a balanced sample of developers
active in every outcome.

Level Regressions The level event study replaces the normalized outcome of Equa-
tion (32) with the raw pair difference:

                       30
                       Õ
𝑌𝑖𝑡 −𝑌𝑖𝑡𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑙 =                𝛽 𝑘 · 1{𝑡 − 𝑡 𝑖∗ = 𝑘} + 𝛾𝑝𝑟𝑒 · 1{𝑡 < 𝑡 𝑖∗ − 11} + 𝛾𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡 · 1{𝑡 > 𝑡 𝑖∗ + 30} + 𝛼 𝑖 + 𝜀𝑖𝑡
                    𝑘=−11,𝑘≠−1
                                                                                         (37)
and is otherwise identical: the same event grid, matched-pair fixed effects, clustering, and
estimation sample. Because level effects vary strongly with baseline activity, we estimate
this level treatment effect separately for each pre-period-mean decile—allowing the effect
to differ across baseline-activity groups—then normalize each decile-specific estimate by
that decile’s treated pre-mean, and finally average across deciles. Figures OA-14 and OA-15
report the decile-level event studies for autocomplete and the pooled sync effect, the final
row of Figure OA-12 reports their average, and Table OA-9 reports the decile estimates
and their averages across the production hierarchy for the pooled sync effect. The pooled


                                                      OA - 49
normalized effect is very similar to the effect one would obtain from averaging the decile-
specific effects, and the pattern of effects attenuating across the production hierarchy is
robust across deciles. The main imperfection appears in the top two deciles of the pooled
sync effect (the final two panels of Figure OA-15): treated developers’ commits rise relative
to their matched controls’ over the weeks leading into adoption. This is precisely where
matching is hardest: controls are drawn by nearest-neighbor matching on pre-period
commits without replacement, and the donor pool thins out at the very top of the activity
distribution, so the most active adopters receive the least comparable matches. Because
level differences scale with activity, proportionally small mismatches that are invisible in
the lower deciles become visible in the top ones. The lower deciles, where matches are
close, show no such pre-trend.

Winsorization Our baseline winsorizes the treated-minus-control difference at the 1st
and 99th percentiles. We re-estimate the event studies winsorizing at the 0.1% level instead.
We choose 0.1% winsorization rather than no winsorization at all because the outcome
distributions contain a few very large outliers that would otherwise entirely dominate
the estimates. The results are reported in Figure OA-12 and Table OA-7: the estimates
are somewhat larger, as expected when less of the thick upper tail is trimmed, but the
dynamics and the patterns across tools are unchanged, and Figure OA-13 shows that the
attenuation across the production hierarchy is still present under this specification.

Activity Window Our baseline censors weeks outside each developer’s active span (Ap-
pendix D.3). We re-estimate the event studies without this restriction. The results are
reported in Figure OA-12 and Table OA-7 and are broadly similar to the baseline; the
attenuation across the production hierarchy is likewise preserved (Figure OA-13).

Balanced Sample Because activity intensity differs across outcomes, the estimation sam-
ple differs across outcomes as well: each outcome drops the pairs with no pre-period
activity in that outcome, and, for example, some developers never open pull requests.
This is most pronounced for releases, which are rare events shipped by only a small frac-
tion of developers. Table OA-6 shows the resulting sample sizes, which are markedly
lower for pull requests and releases. To test whether the cross-outcome comparisons are
driven by these sample differences, we re-estimate all layers on a single set of developers
active in every outcome: Panel A of Table OA-8 restricts to matched pairs present in all
six outcome samples, and Panel B to pairs present in the five samples from lines changed
to repositories touched, excluding releases, because conditioning on releases reduces the
sample the most. The results are robust and the attenuation pattern across the production
hierarchy is preserved. The effect sizes are somewhat smaller, as expected: conditioning


                                          OA - 50
on activity in every outcome excludes less active developers, whose percentage effects are
larger (Table OA-9).

E.3   Ruling Out Activity Bias with Placebos

A central concern with our estimates is activity bias: that the observed effects capture peri-
ods of heightened engagement that coincide with adoption rather than a true productivity
effect from the tool. We address this concern with two pieces of evidence: a comparison
to non-AI coding tools, and an examination of the long-term stability of the AI treatment
effects.

Comparison to Non-AI Coding Tools We evaluate two additional coding tools in Figure 6
as placebo and bounding exercises.
    In the left panel, we study the adoption of GitHub Pro, a paid subscription that provides
advanced collaboration and compute features but no AI capabilities. GitHub Pro is of
interest because willingness to pay may signal periods of heightened engagement with
the platform, potentially contaminating estimates with activity bias. Consistent with this
concern, Figure 6(a) shows a sharp but short-lived increase in activity for GitHub Pro
adopters relative to the control group. This effect dissipates quickly, and in the long run
we estimate no impact of a GitHub Pro subscription on developer productivity. This
suggests focusing on the long-run estimates from our headline event studies.
    In the right panel, we examine the adoption of Docker, a widely used open-source
framework that allows developers to create containers that precisely replicate their de-
velopment environment. By improving reproducibility, Docker may plausibly increase
collaboration efficiency. We study Docker adoption because it is identified in a manner
very similar to the adoption of Claude Code: in both cases, adoption is inferred from the
appearance of a specific configuration file committed to a repository, differing only in the
file’s name. In the absence of any treatment effect, Docker would therefore serve as an ideal
placebo. Since Docker itself likely has a positive productivity effect, however, it is more
appropriately interpreted as providing an upper bound on the magnitude of coefficients
we may see in our headline event studies in the absence of a real effect.
    As shown in Figure 6(b), Docker adoption is associated with a large short-run increase
in activity, which may reflect either temporary activity bias or the resolution of a blocking
technical constraint. This effect declines rapidly, however. While the productivity gains
from coding agents stabilize at levels of around 153.2%, the Docker effect stabilizes at
approximately 6.6%. Hence, even if Docker had no productivity effect and this entire
treatment effect were due to activity bias—an unlikely proposition—we would still infer a
146.6% increase in productivity from the adoption of coding agents.

                                           OA - 51
   Table OA-10 reports the same outcomes across production layers for GitHub Pro and
Docker and finds no attenuation: the placebo effects are small and show no systematic
decline across layers.

Long-Term Stability of the Effect To the extent that the effects in Table 5 are driven by
activity bias, we would expect this bias to be short-lived: in our placebo exercises, the
initial spike in activity dissipates within roughly 10 weeks (see Figure 6). It is therefore
reassuring that the treatment effects from coding agents remain stable over 30 weeks, either
settling down (for the async agents) or strengthening over time (for the sync agents), as
can be seen by comparing the effects in columns “Weeks 11–20” and “Weeks 21–30”.

E.4   External Validity: Public vs. Private Repositories

Our analysis relies on publicly observable activity to identify adoption of AI coding tools.
A natural concern is that we may miss developers who use these tools exclusively on private
repositories. Using internal data from GitHub on async agent usage in November 2025, we
assess the extent of this undercount. We find that our public-repository-based adoption
measure identifies 24.2% of all async agent users—the remainder use the tool only on
private repositories. However, for the developers we do identify as adopters, we observe
72.9% of their usage activity publicly; the remaining activity occurs on private repositories
we do not have access to. In other words, our count of adopters is an undercount, which
is why we do not emphasize adoption numbers. But for the adopters in our sample, their
public activity captures the large majority of their usage, suggesting that our productivity
estimates are based on a representative picture of these developers’ workflows.
    A second piece of evidence comes from repository identifiers. GitHub assigns each
repository a numeric id at creation, and these identifiers are incremental, so the largest id
assigned as of a given date counts every repository ever created on the platform, private
ones included. Enumerating the repositories we can retrieve through the public API gives
the corresponding count of public repositories, and comparing the two implies that 38%
of all repositories ever created on GitHub are public. Because deleted repositories retain
their identifiers and so remain in the denominator, this figure is a lower bound on the
public share. Public activity therefore accounts for a substantial share of the repositories
on the platform, so the data we observe are far from a narrow slice of developer work.

E.5   Robustness to Activity Deletion

Because we collect data through API calls, we observe only the activity still available
on GitHub at collection time. Coding activity can disappear from this record when the
account that produced it is deactivated or the repository that hosts it is deleted or made

                                          OA - 52
 private.
    One alternative is GHArchive, which records daily snapshots of the public GitHub
 event stream going back more than a decade and so preserves activity that has since been
 removed. Our exploration of these data, however, points to quality problems in recent
 years. Figure OA-18 reports the number of push events recorded in GHArchive over time
 and shows clear instability after mid-2024 and a pronounced decline in 2025.49 For this
 reason we do not use GHArchive.
     To gauge how much deletion matters, we instead measure it directly: we download the
 full set of users and repositories on GitHub at two dates ten days apart and track how many
 become unavailable between the two. On an annualized basis, 0.4% of users deactivate
 their accounts and 5.8% of repositories are deleted. These figures likely overstate the
 amount of activity we lose, since deleted accounts and repositories tend to be less active
 than those that persist.




49This is likely attributable to GitHub’s reduction of the data retention window for the Events API, Atom
  feed, /timeline, and /dashboard-feed from 90 days to 30 days, announced in November 2024 and effective
  January 30, 2025 (GitHub Changelog). GHArchive users have separately documented a sharp drop in
  recorded events beginning in May 2025 (GHArchive issue #310).


                                                OA - 53
 F    Time-Shifted Event Studies: Identifying Assumptions
 Because AI coding tools may be adopted without leaving a trace in public repositories, a
 contemporaneous control group risks contamination by private adoption. Same-year non-
 adopters are also likely selected on their future trajectory: developers who forgo a widely
 adopted tool are disproportionately those winding down active development. Our main
 design therefore compares each treated developer to a matched control observed one year
 earlier (Section D). This appendix states the assumptions under which this time-shifted
 event study identifies the treatment effect.
    Let 𝑡 𝑖∗ denote treated developer 𝑖’s adoption week and 𝑐(𝑖) the matched control, whose
                              𝑝                                       e𝑗,𝑡 = 𝑌𝑗,𝑡 /𝑌¯ 𝑝𝑟𝑒 for the
 pseudo-adoption week is 𝑡 𝑖 = 𝑡 𝑖∗ − 𝐿, where 𝐿 is one year. Write 𝑌                𝑗
 normalized outcome that Equation (32) uses, and suppose it follows

                         e𝑗,𝑡 = 𝜇 𝑗 + 𝑔 𝑗 𝑡 + 𝜆(𝑡) + 𝐵(𝑡 − 𝑒 𝑗 ) + 𝜏𝑡−𝑒 𝑗 𝐷 𝑗,𝑡 + 𝜀 𝑗,𝑡 ,
                         𝑌

 where 𝜇 𝑗 is a developer fixed effect, 𝑔 𝑗 a developer-specific linear trend, 𝜆(𝑡) a common
 calendar-time component, and 𝜏𝑘 the treatment effect at event time 𝑘. We state the com-
 ponents in normalized units: a common 𝜆 or 𝐵 then means a shock proportional to a
 developer’s scale, and the cancellations below hold for every outcome, not only those
 where matching equalizes the pair’s pre-period means. The term 𝐵(𝑡 − 𝑒 𝑗 ) is a profile
                                                                             𝑝
 in time relative to 𝑗’s (pseudo-)adoption date 𝑒 𝑗 (𝑒 𝑖 = 𝑡 𝑖∗ , 𝑒 𝑐(𝑖) = 𝑡 𝑖 ): it captures “activity
 bias,” i.e. selection on activity around the event date, such as mean reversion after the
 burst of activity that triggers adoption. Our estimator regresses the time-shifted differ-
 ence Δ𝑖,𝑡 = 𝑌e𝑖,𝑡 − 𝑌
                     e𝑐(𝑖), 𝑡−𝐿 (the dependent variable 𝑌     e𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑙 of Equation (32), written in
                                                        e𝑖𝑡 − 𝑌
                                                               𝑖𝑡
 calendar time) on event-time indicators with pair fixed effects. Substituting,

         Δ𝑖,𝑡 = (𝜇𝑖 − 𝜇𝑐(𝑖) ) + 𝑔𝑐(𝑖) 𝐿 + (𝑔𝑖 − 𝑔𝑐(𝑖) ) 𝑡 + 𝜆(𝑡) − 𝜆(𝑡 − 𝐿) + 𝜏𝑡−𝑡 𝑖∗ 𝐷𝑖,𝑡 + 𝜀˜ 𝑖,𝑡 .
                                                                                 
                  |        {z           }    |                {z                  }
                      absorbed by 𝛼 𝑖                        ≡ 𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡)


 Since treated and control sit at the same event time 𝑘 = 𝑡 −𝑡 𝑖∗ , the profile 𝐵(𝑘) differences out
 exactly—provided it is common to both groups, which our matching targets by requiring
 controls to be active in the pseudo-adoption week and pairing on pre-period activity.50
 What remains is 𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡): in a balanced panel, plim b  𝛽 𝑘 = 𝜏𝑘 + E[𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡 𝑖∗ + 𝑘)] − E[𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡 𝑖∗ − 1)], so
 the design is unbiased when E[𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡 𝑖∗ + 𝑘)] is constant in 𝑘—an expectation the pre-period
 coefficients reveal for 𝑘 < 0. Because each pair is compared only to its own pre-period
50Also recall that our estimates are robust to enforcing identical adoption-week activity requirements on both
  groups (Appendix E.1).



                                                    OA - 54
 and controls remain untreated, the “forbidden comparisons” of staggered two-way fixed-
 effects estimators do not arise (Goodman-Bacon, 2021; Sun and Abraham, 2021). We next
 consider the ways in which 𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡), or the profile 𝐵(·), may fail to difference out, and organize
 them by what the pre-period coefficients would reveal: (i) cases that leave the estimator
 unbiased; (ii) cases that bias it but that the pre-trend test detects; and (iii) cases that bias it
 while leaving the pre-period undisturbed. Only the third threatens the design.
     No bias. Annual seasonality, 𝜆(𝑡) = 𝜆(𝑡 − 𝐿), drops out of 𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡) exactly—the reason we
 shift by precisely one year. A common linear trend, 𝜆(𝑡) = 𝑐𝑡, leaves 𝜆(𝑡) − 𝜆(𝑡 − 𝐿) = 𝑐𝐿,
 absorbed by the pair fixed effect.
     Bias detected by the pre-trend test. Because adoption is staggered over many months,
 shocks on fixed calendar dates typically fall after adoption for early adopters but within the
 eleven-week pre-period of later ones, whose pre-period coefficients then move: calendar-
 time variation in 𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡) generally loads onto the pre-period, and the test rejects in the
 infinite-data limit. This covers an accelerating common trend (𝜆(𝑡) = 𝑐𝑡 2 , so 𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡) is linear
 in 𝑡), aggregate shocks at fixed calendar dates, systematically different treated and control
 trends (E[𝑔𝑖 − 𝑔𝑐(𝑖) ] ≠ 0), a slope break in the common trend, and anticipation of adoption.
 The qualification is that the variation must fall within the pre-period of at least some
 adopters; a shock arriving only after all adoption dates (after 2025-12-22 for the tools
 launched in 2025; see Table OA-3) escapes the test.
      Undetectable bias. The pre-trend test is silent about components of 𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡), or asymmetries
 in 𝐵(·), that emerge only at or after adoption; the identifying assumption is that there
 are none. Four cases are worth noting. First, a late aggregate shock occurring after all
 adoptions.51 Second, matched controls may adopt an AI tool during the weeks serving
 as the treated developer’s post-period, so that their treatment effect is subtracted from
 Δ𝑖,𝑡 ; this attenuates b𝛽 𝑘 toward zero—making our estimates conservative—and is limited
 by drawing controls from the year before adoption, when these tools were less available.
 Third, developers may adopt at the start of a new project or burst of work, so that treated
 activity would have risen at 𝑡 𝑖∗ even absent treatment—an asymmetry in 𝐵(·) arising exactly
 at 𝑘 = 0. The non-AI placebo tools in Figure 6 show the footprint of such residual selection:
 a sharp spike at adoption that decays within weeks, far from the persistent effects we
 estimate for AI tools. Finally, any shock to treated developers coinciding with adoption
 itself is indistinguishable from a treatment effect—the classic violation of counterfactual
 parallel trends, to which no difference-in-differences design is robust.
51Calendar-week fixed effects—separately identified from event time because adoption is staggered—would
  absorb any fixed-date shock. However, if private adoption is widespread in the control group, it likely also
  occurs among treated developers before their public adoption, so this repair is ultimately fruitless.



                                                   OA - 55
    In sum, relative to a standard difference-in-differences, the time-shifted design trades
robustness to aggregate shocks occurring after the last-observed adoption for robustness
to contamination and to selection of non-adopters on future engagement, while relying on
the same fundamental requirement of counterfactual parallel trends.

coefficients are 1131.3%, 322.8%, 247.0%, −16.8%, 7.4%, and 3.8% for Panels OA-8(a)–OA-8(f), respectively.
